# Implicit Geometry of Next-token Prediction: From Language Sparsity Patterns to Model Representations

Yize Zhao†, Tina Behnia†, Vala Vakilian & Christos Thrampoulidis
  
Department of Electrical & Computer Engineering
  
University of British Columbia
  
Vancouver, Canada
  
{zhaoyize,tina.behnia,vaalaa,cthrampo}@ece.ubc.ca
  
† Equal contribution.

###### Abstract

Next-token prediction (NTP) over large text corpora has become the go-to paradigm to train large language models. Yet, it remains unclear how NTP influences the mapping of linguistic patterns to geometric properties of the resulting model representations. We frame training of large language models as soft-label classification over sparse probabilistic label vectors, coupled with an analytical approximation that allows unrestricted generation of context embeddings. This approach links NTP training to rank-constrained, nuclear-norm regularized optimization in the logit domain, offering a framework for analyzing the geometry of word and context embeddings. In large embedding spaces, we find that NTP implicitly favors learning logits with a sparse plus low-rank structure. While the sparse component captures the co-occurrence frequency of context-word pairs, the orthogonal low-rank component, which becomes dominant as training progresses, depends solely on the sparsity pattern of the co-occurrence matrix. Consequently, when projected onto an appropriate subspace, representations of contexts that are followed by the same set of next-tokens collapse—a phenomenon we term subspace-collapse. We validate our findings on synthetic and small-scale real language datasets. Finally, we outline potential research directions aimed at deepening the understanding of NTP’s influence on the learning of linguistic patterns and regularities.

## 1 Introduction

Next-token prediction (NTP) is the preferred training paradigm for state-of-the-art language models. The process, elegantly simple, uses a large training corpus to minimize, for each context 𝒛<t∈𝒱t−1subscript𝒛absent𝑡superscript𝒱𝑡1\bm{z}\_{<t}\in\mathcal{V}^{t-1} of t−1𝑡1t-1 preceding tokens, the cross-entropy (CE) loss between the model’s predicted conditional probability distribution over potential next tokens from a vocabulary 𝒱𝒱\mathcal{V} and the one-hot encoded actual next token zt∈𝒱subscript𝑧𝑡𝒱z\_{t}\in\mathcal{V}. The model’s conditional distribution is defined through a softmax map applied to logits ℓ<t​(𝑾,𝜽)=𝑾​𝒉𝜽​(𝒛<t)subscriptbold-ℓabsent𝑡𝑾𝜽𝑾subscript𝒉𝜽subscript𝒛absent𝑡\bm{\ell}\_{<t}({\bm{W}},\bm{\theta})={\bm{W}}\bm{h}\_{\bm{\theta}}(\bm{z}\_{<t}), which are generated by mapping *context embeddings* 𝒉𝜽​(𝒛<t)∈ℝdsubscript𝒉𝜽subscript𝒛absent𝑡superscriptℝ𝑑\bm{h}\_{\bm{\theta}}(\bm{z}\_{<t})\in\mathbb{R}^{d}—the neural network’s d𝑑d-dimensional representations of contexts 𝒛<tsubscript𝒛absent𝑡\bm{z}\_{<t}—using a matrix 𝑾∈ℝ|𝒱|×d𝑾superscriptℝ𝒱𝑑{\bm{W}}\in\mathbb{R}^{|\mathcal{V}|\times d} of *word embeddings*.

Rooted in the foundational works of Shannon ([1948](#bib.bib56)) and inspired by the “co-occurrence statistics” and “distributional hypothesis” (Harris, [1954](#bib.bib24)), the NTP paradigm suggests that a word’s meaning is defined by its context. This principle underlies both classical vector-space models (Schütze et al., [2008](#bib.bib55)) and neural language models (Turian et al., [2010](#bib.bib65); Bengio et al., [2000](#bib.bib7); Baroni et al., [2014](#bib.bib4); Bengio & Bengio, [2000](#bib.bib6); Mikolov et al., [2013b](#bib.bib40)), propelling the development of today’s sophisticated large language models (Radford et al., [2018](#bib.bib50); [2019](#bib.bib51); Brown et al., [2020](#bib.bib9)). However, a fundamental question remains:

*How does the NTP learning objective shape the relationship between
  
language statistics and the geometry of model representations?*

This inquiry, which we term the *implicit geometry* of NTP—so named because the NTP objective does not explicitly impose any such relationship—explores how distances and angles within the neural network’s d𝑑d-dimensional representational space correlate with linguistic patterns at the end of training.

We postulate that understanding this implicit geometry is key to understanding functional principles of large language models, since NTP is used for training across diverse architectures, from LSTMs to transformers and state-space models. Specifically, exploring how optimization under NTP shapes representations of words and contexts, which empirically mirror complex human-like patterns, not only fosters scientific interest but could also enhance model interpretability and explainability. Further, revealing how implicit geometry correlates with language statistics could lead to refined training and inference methods, addressing challenges like statistical imbalances in language data. Conversely, understanding how state-of-the-art models internalize language to form representations might also enhance our grasp of language itself.

This paper develops an analytical framework to characterize the implicit geometry induced by NTP training on language datasets. Drawing inspiration from seminal studies on the geometry of deep model representations in image recognition (Papyan et al., [2020](#bib.bib46)), our framework distinguishes itself from previous studies on language representations by not concentrating on specific architectures such as transformers. Instead, we assume that the model has adequate representation capacity and undergoes effective optimization, making it possible to minimize the NTP loss to its entropy lower-bound. This approach isolates the influence of NTP itself—rather than architectural nuances—in shaping the implicit geometry of the language model.

The framework reveals the key role of the sparsity pattern in language statistics on the implicit geometry (Thrampoulidis, [2024](#bib.bib62)). Concretely, we demonstrate that the recurrence of only a few tokens from the entire vocabulary as next-tokens in specific contexts leads to an implicit bias in NTP training. This bias favors a matrix of logits that develops a *sparse plus low-rank* structure during training.
The sparse component of this matrix captures the probabilities of co-occurring words and contexts, while the dominant low-rank component reflects the sparsity pattern of the co-occurrence matrix. Consequently, embeddings of contexts followed by similar sets of words—regardless of their frequencies—increasingly align during training. Simultaneously, the sparse component of the logits ensures accurate prediction of the correct frequencies for each context, which is crucial for achieving the entropy lower bound.
Overall, this framework introduces a novel perspective, distinct from traditional analyses of word representations like those in the Word2Vec model (Levy & Goldberg, [2014](#bib.bib33)).

### 1.1 Methodology

Our methodology integrates three foundational modeling concepts as follows:

Firstly, following (Thrampoulidis, [2024](#bib.bib62)) we frame NTP as soft-label classification with CE loss applied to *sparse* probabilistic label vectors. This isolates m𝑚m *distinct* contexts, which could be repeated multiple times throughout the training corpus, and assigns to each a sparse V=|𝒱|𝑉𝒱V=|\mathcal{V}|-dimensional conditional-probability label vector 𝒑^jsubscript^𝒑𝑗\hat{\bm{p}}\_{j}, reflecting the frequency of each token following context j∈[m]𝑗delimited-[]𝑚j\in[m]. The sparsity of 𝒑^jsubscript^𝒑𝑗\hat{\bm{p}}\_{j} indicates that certain tokens, which we refer to as *off-support* tokens for the specific j𝑗j-th context, never follow this context.

The second concept facilitates a tractable analysis of context embeddings by assuming expressive (enough) neural networks can produce unconstrained embeddings 𝒉j∈ℝdsubscript𝒉𝑗superscriptℝ𝑑\bm{h}\_{j}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}, independent of the architecture’s specific complexities (Yang et al., [2017](#bib.bib69); Mixon et al., [2020](#bib.bib42)). This redefines NTP as a minimization, over word and context embedding matrices 𝑾𝑾{\bm{W}} and 𝑯𝑯{\bm{H}}, of the NTP loss across a training set 𝒯𝒯\mathcal{T} determined by the matrix of conditional probabilities 𝑷=𝑷​(𝒯)=[𝒑^1,𝒑^2,…,𝒑^m]∈[0,1]V×m𝑷𝑷𝒯

subscript^𝒑1subscript^𝒑2…subscript^𝒑𝑚superscript01𝑉𝑚{\bm{P}}={\bm{P}}(\mathcal{T})=\left[\hat{\bm{p}}\_{1},\hat{\bm{p}}\_{2},\ldots,\hat{\bm{p}}\_{m}\right]\in[0,1]^{V\times m}, leading to the following log-bilinear model:

|  |  |  |  |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
|  | min𝑾∈ℝV×d,𝑯∈ℝd×m⁡ℒNTP​(𝑾​𝑯;𝑷).subscriptformulae-sequence𝑾superscriptℝ𝑉𝑑𝑯superscriptℝ𝑑𝑚subscriptℒNTP  𝑾𝑯𝑷\displaystyle\min\nolimits\_{{\bm{W}}\in\mathbb{R}^{V\times d},\,{\bm{H}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times m}}\,\mathcal{L}\_{\rm{NTP}}\big{(}{\bm{W}}{\bm{H}};{\bm{P}}\big{)}\,.\vspace{-2pt} |  | (1) |

This way, our goal to study word-word, context-context, and word-context geometric relationships becomes that of characterizing the Gram matrices 𝑮𝑾=𝑾​𝑾⊤subscript𝑮𝑾𝑾superscript𝑾top\bm{G}\_{\bm{W}}={\bm{W}}{\bm{W}}^{\top} and 𝑮𝑯=𝑯⊤​𝑯subscript𝑮𝑯superscript𝑯top𝑯\bm{G}\_{{\bm{H}}}={\bm{H}}^{\top}{\bm{H}}, as well as the logit matrix 𝑳=𝑾​𝑯𝑳𝑾𝑯\bm{L}={\bm{W}}{\bm{H}} at the minimizers of Eq. ([1](#S1.E1 "In 1.1 Methodology ‣ 1 Introduction ‣ Implicit Geometry of Next-token Prediction: From Language Sparsity Patterns to Model Representations")). This task is complicated by the non-convex nature of the minimization and the sparsity of the probabilistic label vectors. Specifically, as we show, the sparsity of 𝑷𝑷{\bm{P}} may lead to multiple minimizers, potentially making geometric characterization ambiguous.

To address this, we leverage a third concept: focusing on specific minimizers identified through the *regularization path* (Rosset et al., [2003](#bib.bib53)). This entails following the solution trajectory of the empirical risk minimization in Eq. ([1](#S1.E1 "In 1.1 Methodology ‣ 1 Introduction ‣ Implicit Geometry of Next-token Prediction: From Language Sparsity Patterns to Model Representations")) as an additive ridge regularization for 𝑾𝑾{\bm{W}} and 𝑯𝑯{\bm{H}} diminishes to zero. For the purpose of comparing the analysis outcomes to our numerical evaluations, we interpret the prediction obtained from the regularization path analysis as a proxy
for the solution found by gradient-based optimization when the NTP loss approaches its empirical entropy lower bound.

𝑳insuperscript𝑳in\bm{L}^{\rm{in}} inherits the sparsity of the data matrix 𝑷𝑷{\bm{P}} and encodes information regarding frequencies of in-support tokens. In contrast, 𝑳mmsuperscript𝑳mm\bm{L}^{\rm{mm}} is independent of these frequencies and is influenced only by the sparsity pattern 𝑺∈{0,1}V×m𝑺superscript01𝑉𝑚\bm{S}\in\{0,1\}^{V\times m}. Thus, its value is guided by both the observed ‘company’ of each context (in-support tokens), but not their frequency, and, by the ‘company’ it lacks (off-support tokens). More specifically,
𝑳mmsuperscript𝑳mm\bm{L}^{\rm{mm}} functions to maximize the logit margin between in-support and off-support tokens, where the margin is defined in terms of the nuclear norm that promotes low rank, rather than the Euclidean norm. To highlight this property, we refer to
𝑳mmsuperscript𝑳mm\bm{L}^{\rm{mm}}
as the ‘low-rank’ component, though it could also be described as the ‘max-margin’ component. The decomposition in Eq. ([2](#S1.E2 "In item (C1) ‣ 1.2 Summary of findings ‣ 1 Introduction ‣ Implicit Geometry of Next-token Prediction: From Language Sparsity Patterns to Model Representations")) also shows the logits become unbounded in norm when projected on the subspace of 𝑳mmsuperscript𝑳mm\bm{L}^{\rm{mm}}.

Experiments. Sec. [5](#S5 "5 Experiments ‣ Implicit Geometry of Next-token Prediction: From Language Sparsity Patterns to Model Representations") presents experiments on controlled settings that validate our findings.
An example is shown in Fig. [1](#S1.F1 "Figure 1 ‣ 1 Introduction ‣ Implicit Geometry of Next-token Prediction: From Language Sparsity Patterns to Model Representations"): The experiment involves training a 4-layer transformer (TF) on a training set extracted and curated from the TinyStories dataset (Eldan & Li, [2023](#bib.bib13)), with the following characteristics: a vocabulary size V=104𝑉104V=104 and a total number of contexts n≈3050𝑛3050n\approx 3050, out of which m≈400𝑚400m\approx 400 are distinct. We choose TF embedding dimension d=128>V𝑑128𝑉d=128>V and train until the empirical entropy lower bound is reached within an order of 10−4superscript10410^{-4}. The leftmost Panel (a) depicts the geometry of the context embeddings 𝑯𝑯{\bm{H}} (Top), word embeddings 𝑾𝑾{\bm{W}} (Middle), and logits 𝑳𝑳\bm{L} (Bottom) learned by the TF at the end of training.
Panel (b) compares these with the same quantities learned by the log-bilinear model in Eq. ([1](#S1.E1 "In 1.1 Methodology ‣ 1 Introduction ‣ Implicit Geometry of Next-token Prediction: From Language Sparsity Patterns to Model Representations")) evaluated on the same training dataset. The apparent resemblance of the patterns validates that the proposed log-bilinear analysis model is a good proxy for the TF model. Panel (c) compares the TF output to our analytical predictions: Comparing TF’s context/word embeddings and logits to 𝑯mmsuperscript𝑯mm{\bm{H}}^{\rm{mm}}/𝑾mmsuperscript𝑾mm{\bm{W}}^{\rm{mm}} and 𝑳mmsuperscript𝑳mm\bm{L}^{\rm{mm}} confirms Claims [(C2)](#S1.I2.ix1 "item (C2) ‣ 1.2 Summary of findings ‣ 1 Introduction ‣ Implicit Geometry of Next-token Prediction: From Language Sparsity Patterns to Model Representations") and [(C1)](#S1.I1.ix1 "item (C1) ‣ 1.2 Summary of findings ‣ 1 Introduction ‣ Implicit Geometry of Next-token Prediction: From Language Sparsity Patterns to Model Representations").
The bright yellow regions in the Gram matrices of embeddings help visualize the subspace collapse Claim [(C3)](#S1.I2.ix2 "item (C3) ‣ 1.2 Summary of findings ‣ 1 Introduction ‣ Implicit Geometry of Next-token Prediction: From Language Sparsity Patterns to Model Representations"). See Sec. [5](#S5 "5 Experiments ‣ Implicit Geometry of Next-token Prediction: From Language Sparsity Patterns to Model Representations") for additional details and verification of Claim [(C4)](#S1.I2.ix3 "item (C4) ‣ 1.2 Summary of findings ‣ 1 Introduction ‣ Implicit Geometry of Next-token Prediction: From Language Sparsity Patterns to Model Representations").

Finding the analytical prediction [(C2)](#S1.I2.ix1 "item (C2) ‣ 1.2 Summary of findings ‣ 1 Introduction ‣ Implicit Geometry of Next-token Prediction: From Language Sparsity Patterns to Model Representations"), illustrated in Panel (c), requires solving a semi-definite program for 𝑳mmsuperscript𝑳mm\bm{L}^{\rm{mm}} and computing its SVD. In large scales, this can be computationally prohibitive, motivating the following heuristic proxy for the embedding geometry.

1. (P)

   Empirical proxy for directional convergence: Let 𝑺~:=(𝕀V−1V​𝟙V​𝟙V⊤)​𝑺assign~𝑺subscript𝕀𝑉1𝑉subscript1𝑉superscriptsubscript1𝑉top𝑺\widetilde{\bm{S}}:=(\mathds{I}\_{V}-\frac{1}{V}\mathds{1}\_{V}\mathds{1}\_{V}^{\top})\bm{S} be the (column-wise) centered support-set matrix. The directional component of
   word and context embedding matrices can be well-approximated by 𝑺~~𝑺\widetilde{\bm{S}} as follows:

   |  |  |  |  |
   | --- | --- | --- | --- |
   |  | 𝑾mm​𝑾mm⊤≈𝑺~​𝑺~⊤and𝑯mm⊤​𝑯mm≈𝑺~⊤​𝑺~.formulae-sequencesuperscript𝑾mmsuperscriptsuperscript𝑾mmtop ~𝑺superscript~𝑺topandsuperscriptsuperscript𝑯mmtopsuperscript𝑯mmsuperscript~𝑺top~𝑺\displaystyle{\bm{W}}^{\rm{mm}}{{\bm{W}}^{\rm{mm}}}^{\top}\,\approx\,\widetilde{\bm{S}}\widetilde{\bm{S}}^{\top}\quad\text{and}\quad{{\bm{H}}^{\rm{mm}}}^{\top}{\bm{H}}^{\rm{mm}}\,\approx\,\widetilde{\bm{S}}^{\top}\widetilde{\bm{S}}\,. |  | (6) |

Panel (d) of Fig. [1](#S1.F1 "Figure 1 ‣ 1 Introduction ‣ Implicit Geometry of Next-token Prediction: From Language Sparsity Patterns to Model Representations") suggests proxy [(P)](#S1.I3.ix1 "item (P) ‣ 1.2 Summary of findings ‣ 1 Introduction ‣ Implicit Geometry of Next-token Prediction: From Language Sparsity Patterns to Model Representations") as a good enough approximator of the embeddings structure that simply only depends on the sparsity pattern 𝑺𝑺\bm{S} of the next-tokens’ conditional probability matrix.

A second example supporting proxy [(P)](#S1.I3.ix1 "item (P) ‣ 1.2 Summary of findings ‣ 1 Introduction ‣ Implicit Geometry of Next-token Prediction: From Language Sparsity Patterns to Model Representations")
is shown in Fig. [2](#S1.F2 "Figure 2 ‣ 1.2 Summary of findings ‣ 1 Introduction ‣ Implicit Geometry of Next-token Prediction: From Language Sparsity Patterns to Model Representations"): Here, we train a larger TF on a subset of the TinyStories, where computing 𝑳mmsuperscript𝑳mm\bm{L}^{\rm{mm}} and its SVD is expensive. Instead, we observe that the structure of the support set closely captures the structure of the learned embeddings: Along the diagonal blocks, where contexts with the exact same support sets are situated, the context embeddings align closely (subspace collapse in Claim [(C3)](#S1.I2.ix2 "item (C3) ‣ 1.2 Summary of findings ‣ 1 Introduction ‣ Implicit Geometry of Next-token Prediction: From Language Sparsity Patterns to Model Representations")). Conversely, when support sets have zero intersection (dark entries in Panel (b)), context embeddings exhibit low correlation. This finding is consistent with classical intuitions of the “distributional assumption” of words/contexts and is formalized by our framework via the implicit bias of NTP to promote low-rank logits subject to margin conditions for in/off-support tokens.

## 2 Related work

We pinpoint three main related research areas; see App. [A](#A1 "Appendix A Related work: Detailed version ‣ Implicit Geometry of Next-token Prediction: From Language Sparsity Patterns to Model Representations") for more in-depth discussion.

First, our research conceptually mirrors the seminal work by Levy & Goldberg ([2014](#bib.bib33)) who framed the Skip-Gram with Negative Sampling (SGNS) training objective of Word2Vec (Mikolov et al., [2013b](#bib.bib40); [a](#bib.bib39)) as weighted matrix factorization. Specifically for large d𝑑d, they demonstrated that SGNS implicitly factorizes the pointwise mutual information (PMI) matrix. Their analysis relies on the fact that Word2Vec architecture already is a log-biliniear model, while the log-bilinear model in ([1](#S1.E1 "In 1.1 Methodology ‣ 1 Introduction ‣ Implicit Geometry of Next-token Prediction: From Language Sparsity Patterns to Model Representations")) is only used by us as an analytical proxy for more complex architectures.
Also, different to them, we focus on NTP, which employs softmax instead of sigmoids as in SGNS. (However, our analysis applies also to SGNS; see App. [A.4](#A1.SS4 "A.4 Connections to Word2Vec ‣ Appendix A Related work: Detailed version ‣ Implicit Geometry of Next-token Prediction: From Language Sparsity Patterns to Model Representations")). More importantly, we contribute a fresh perspective on this line of inquiry by: (i) confronting the sparsity of probabilistic labels head-on, identifying that it leads to diverging embeddings, a scenario where setting the loss gradient to zero, as in Levy & Goldberg ([2014](#bib.bib33)), is infeasible; (ii) examining embeddings through the lens of the regularization path—a surrogate for gradient descent optimization— which unveils that embeddings emerge from the factorization of
𝑳in+R​𝑳mmsuperscript𝑳in𝑅superscript𝑳mm\bm{L}^{\rm{in}}+R\bm{L}^{\rm{mm}}, where
𝑳insuperscript𝑳in\bm{L}^{\rm{in}} captures frequencies akin to the PMI, while the directional component
𝑳mmsuperscript𝑳mm\bm{L}^{\rm{mm}}, becoming dominant as weights diverge, reflects the explicit sparsity patterns of the context-word co-occurrences. We envision this fresh perspective could similarly motivate further research leveraging the geometric insights of embeddings to uncover linguistic phenomena such as the linear relationships underlying word analogies (Mikolov et al., [2013a](#bib.bib39); Pennington et al., [2014](#bib.bib47)).

Second, our exploration of NTP’s regularization path is inspired by the *implicit bias/regularization* research on the preferred solutions of optimizers like GD in overparameterized systems. Ji & Telgarsky ([2020](#bib.bib30)) showed that GD’s trajectory in linear one-hot encoding models aligns with the regularization path, providing a lens for examining GD dynamics. Thrampoulidis ([2024](#bib.bib62)) recently extended this analysis to NTP, framing it as sparse soft-label classification. By lifting their assumption of fixed context embeddings, we delve into the more complex non-convex domain, establishing explicit connections between the implicit geometry and linguistic patterns, which we also verify empirically.

Finally, our research intersects with the study of *neural-collapse (NC)*, which delves into the geometry of last-layer features and weights in deep networks trained in the interpolating regime (Papyan et al., [2020](#bib.bib46)), utilizing the unconstrained festures model (UFM) for analysis (Mixon et al., [2020](#bib.bib42); Fang et al., [2021](#bib.bib16); Zhu et al., [2021](#bib.bib70)). Our work is particularly aligned with Thrampoulidis et al. ([2022](#bib.bib63)): We also examine the UFM’s regularization path but within the NTP framework, extending their one-hot classification findings, which can be seen as special cases of ours. Additionally, our findings have some parallels with the multilabel NC geometry explored in Li et al. ([2023](#bib.bib35)); Fisher et al. ([2024](#bib.bib18)). However, our
setting is more general raising stringent assumptions on the label distribution. For detailed comparison, see App. [A](#A1 "Appendix A Related work: Detailed version ‣ Implicit Geometry of Next-token Prediction: From Language Sparsity Patterns to Model Representations").

|  |  |  |  |

|  | CE⁡(𝜽′)=−∑j∈[m]π^j​∑z∈𝒱p^j,z​log⁡(𝕊z​(𝑾​𝒉𝜽​(𝒙¯j))).CEsuperscript𝜽′subscript𝑗delimited-[]𝑚subscript^𝜋𝑗subscript𝑧𝒱subscript^𝑝  𝑗𝑧subscript𝕊𝑧𝑾subscript𝒉𝜽subscript¯𝒙𝑗\displaystyle\operatorname{CE}({\bm{\theta}^{\prime}})=-\sum\nolimits\_{j\in[m]}\hat{\pi}\_{j}\sum\nolimits\_{z\in\mathcal{V}}\hat{p}\_{j,z}\log\left(\mathbb{S}\_{z}({\bm{W}}\bm{h}\_{\bm{\theta}}(\bar{\bm{x}}\_{j}))\right)\,. |  | (7) |

This is lower bounded by the empirical T𝑇T-gram entropy (referred to hereafter as entropy) of the data (Shannon, [1948](#bib.bib56)), i.e., for all 𝜽′superscript𝜽′\bm{\theta}^{\prime}:

|  |  |  |
| --- | --- | --- |
|  | CE⁡(𝜽′)≥ℋ:=−∑j∈[m]π^j​∑z∈𝒱p^j,z​log⁡(p^j,z).CEsuperscript𝜽′ℋassignsubscript𝑗delimited-[]𝑚subscript^𝜋𝑗subscript𝑧𝒱subscript^𝑝  𝑗𝑧subscript^𝑝  𝑗𝑧\operatorname{CE}(\bm{\theta}^{\prime})\geq\mathcal{H}:=-\sum\nolimits\_{j\in[m]}\hat{\pi}\_{j}\sum\nolimits\_{z\in\mathcal{V}}\hat{p}\_{j,z}\log\left(\hat{p}\_{j,z}\right). |  |

### 3.2 Unconstrained features model for NTP training

To gain insights into the geometry of solutions to CE minimization in Eq. ([7](#S3.E7 "In 3.1 NTP objective as soft-label classification ‣ 3 Formulation ‣ Implicit Geometry of Next-token Prediction: From Language Sparsity Patterns to Model Representations")), we assume sufficient model expressivity, allowing us to optimize embeddings freely, instead of abiding by their architecture-specific parameterization. We formalize this concept below.

###### Definition 1 (NTP-UFM).

The unconstrained features model (UFM) for NTP training over training set 𝒯m={π^j,𝐩^j}j∈[m]subscript𝒯𝑚subscriptsubscript^𝜋𝑗subscript^𝐩𝑗𝑗delimited-[]𝑚\mathcal{T}\_{m}=\{\hat{\pi}\_{j},\hat{\bm{p}}\_{j}\}\_{j\in[m]} refers to the following log-bilinear optimization problem:

|  |  |  |  |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
|  | min𝑾,𝑯⁡CE⁡(𝑾​𝑯)+λ2​‖𝑾‖2+λ2​‖𝑯‖2,subscript  𝑾𝑯CE𝑾𝑯𝜆2superscriptnorm𝑾2𝜆2superscriptnorm𝑯2\displaystyle\min\nolimits\_{{\bm{W}},{\bm{H}}}~{}~{}\operatorname{CE}({\bm{W}}{\bm{H}})+\frac{{\lambda}}{2}\|{\bm{W}}\|^{2}+\frac{{\lambda}}{2}\|{\bm{H}}\|^{2}, |  | (NTP-UFM) |

where minimization is over word and context embedding matrices 𝐖∈ℝV×d𝐖superscriptℝ𝑉𝑑{\bm{W}}\in\mathbb{R}^{V\times d} and 𝐇:=[𝐡1,…,𝐡m]∈ℝd×massign𝐇

subscript𝐡1…subscript𝐡𝑚superscriptℝ𝑑𝑚{\bm{H}}:=[\bm{h}\_{1},\ldots,\bm{h}\_{m}]\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times m}, and
CE⁡(𝐖​𝐇):=−∑j∈[m]π^j​∑z∈𝒮jp^j,z​log⁡(𝕊z​(𝐖​𝐡j)).assignCE𝐖𝐇subscript𝑗delimited-[]𝑚subscript^𝜋𝑗subscript𝑧subscript𝒮𝑗subscript^𝑝

𝑗𝑧subscript𝕊𝑧𝐖subscript𝐡𝑗\operatorname{CE}({\bm{W}}{\bm{H}}):=-\sum\_{j\in[m]}\hat{\pi}\_{j}\sum\_{z\in{\mathcal{S}}\_{j}}\hat{p}\_{j,z}\log\left(\mathbb{S}\_{z}({\bm{W}}\bm{h}\_{j})\right)\,.

The key difference of [NTP-UFM](#S3.Ex4 "In Definition 1 (NTP-UFM). ‣ 3.2 Unconstrained features model for NTP training ‣ 3 Formulation ‣ Implicit Geometry of Next-token Prediction: From Language Sparsity Patterns to Model Representations") compared to Eq. ([7](#S3.E7 "In 3.1 NTP objective as soft-label classification ‣ 3 Formulation ‣ Implicit Geometry of Next-token Prediction: From Language Sparsity Patterns to Model Representations")) is that embeddings 𝒉𝜽​(𝒙¯j)subscript𝒉𝜽subscript¯𝒙𝑗\bm{h}\_{\bm{\theta}}(\bar{\bm{x}}\_{j}) are now optimized unconstrainedly through free variables 𝒉jsubscript𝒉𝑗\bm{h}\_{j}. Furthermore, we have introduced ridge regularization.222For simplicity, we apply equal regularization to 𝑾𝑾{\bm{W}} and 𝑯𝑯{\bm{H}}. While our results can easily extend to the general scenario, such extensions do not yield additional insights.
Although our focus is on understanding the geometry arising from minimizing the unregularized CE objective in Eq. ([7](#S3.E7 "In 3.1 NTP objective as soft-label classification ‣ 3 Formulation ‣ Implicit Geometry of Next-token Prediction: From Language Sparsity Patterns to Model Representations")), we utilize ridge regularization as a proxy to examine the behavior of gradient descent.333The equivalence between GD and regularization paths has been rigorously established in certain convex settings (Ji et al., [2020](#bib.bib31)), but it may not hold generally. Our setting is non-convex, so we use this equivalence as a heuristic proxy; see future work discussion in Sec. [6](#S6 "6 Concluding remarks: Limitations and future work ‣ Implicit Geometry of Next-token Prediction: From Language Sparsity Patterns to Model Representations").
Specifically, we use the vanishing-regularization solution of [NTP-UFM](#S3.Ex4 "In Definition 1 (NTP-UFM). ‣ 3.2 Unconstrained features model for NTP training ‣ 3 Formulation ‣ Implicit Geometry of Next-token Prediction: From Language Sparsity Patterns to Model Representations") as a proxy for the parameters (𝑾k,𝑯k)subscript𝑾𝑘subscript𝑯𝑘({\bm{W}}\_{k},{\bm{H}}\_{k}) learned in large iteration k→∞→𝑘k\rightarrow\infty through GD on the unregularized objective ([7](#S3.E7 "In 3.1 NTP objective as soft-label classification ‣ 3 Formulation ‣ Implicit Geometry of Next-token Prediction: From Language Sparsity Patterns to Model Representations")).

###### Definition 2 (Regularization path).

The regularization path of the [NTP-UFM](#S3.Ex4 "In Definition 1 (NTP-UFM). ‣ 3.2 Unconstrained features model for NTP training ‣ 3 Formulation ‣ Implicit Geometry of Next-token Prediction: From Language Sparsity Patterns to Model Representations") minimization corresponds to the solution (𝐖λ,𝐇λ)subscript𝐖𝜆subscript𝐇𝜆({\bm{W}}\_{\lambda},{\bm{H}}\_{\lambda}) of the respective log-bilinear program in the limit of vanishing regularization λ→0→𝜆0{\lambda}\rightarrow 0.

This is grounded on the well-known fact concerning the nuclear norm of a matrix (Srebro et al., [2004](#bib.bib58); Fazel, [2002](#bib.bib17)):‖𝑳‖∗=min𝑳=𝑾​𝑯⁡12​‖𝑾‖2+12​‖𝑯‖2subscriptnorm𝑳subscript𝑳𝑾𝑯12superscriptnorm𝑾212superscriptnorm𝑯2\|\bm{L}\|\_{\*}=\min\_{\bm{L}={\bm{W}}{\bm{H}}}\frac{1}{2}\|{\bm{W}}\|^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\|{\bm{H}}\|^{2}\,.

Thus, in general, Eq. ([8](#S3.E8 "In Lemma 1. ‣ 3.3 Reformulation in terms of logits ‣ 3 Formulation ‣ Implicit Geometry of Next-token Prediction: From Language Sparsity Patterns to Model Representations")) is non-convex.

## 4 Analysis of unconstrained features model for NTP training

This section analyzes the regularization path of Eq. ([8](#S3.E8 "In Lemma 1. ‣ 3.3 Reformulation in terms of logits ‣ 3 Formulation ‣ Implicit Geometry of Next-token Prediction: From Language Sparsity Patterns to Model Representations")) and, subsequently, of [NTP-UFM](#S3.Ex4 "In Definition 1 (NTP-UFM). ‣ 3.2 Unconstrained features model for NTP training ‣ 3 Formulation ‣ Implicit Geometry of Next-token Prediction: From Language Sparsity Patterns to Model Representations"). Throughout, we assume d≥V𝑑𝑉d\geq V,444This can be slightly improved to d≥V−1𝑑𝑉1d\geq V-1: Strong convexity of ridge-regularization and translational invariance of softmax imply any minimizer 𝑾^^𝑾\widehat{{\bm{W}}} of [NTP-UFM](#S3.Ex4 "In Definition 1 (NTP-UFM). ‣ 3.2 Unconstrained features model for NTP training ‣ 3 Formulation ‣ Implicit Geometry of Next-token Prediction: From Language Sparsity Patterns to Model Representations") is centered, i.e., 𝟙V⊤​𝑾λ=0superscriptsubscript1𝑉topsubscript𝑾𝜆0\mathds{1}\_{V}^{\top}{\bm{W}}\_{\lambda}=0. This implies 𝟙V⊤​𝑳=0superscriptsubscript1𝑉top𝑳0\mathds{1}\_{V}^{\top}\bm{L}=0 can be added in Eq. ([8](#S3.E8 "In Lemma 1. ‣ 3.3 Reformulation in terms of logits ‣ 3 Formulation ‣ Implicit Geometry of Next-token Prediction: From Language Sparsity Patterns to Model Representations")) without changing Lemma [1](#Thmlemma1 "Lemma 1. ‣ 3.3 Reformulation in terms of logits ‣ 3 Formulation ‣ Implicit Geometry of Next-token Prediction: From Language Sparsity Patterns to Model Representations").
in which case the constraint rank⁡(𝑳)≤drank𝑳𝑑\operatorname{rank}\left(\bm{L}\right)\leq d becomes redundant. All proofs are deferred to the appendix and numerical evaluations to Sec. [4.5](#S4.SS5 "4.5 Numerical simulation: Verifying the theory ‣ 4 Analysis of unconstrained features model for NTP training ‣ Implicit Geometry of Next-token Prediction: From Language Sparsity Patterns to Model Representations").

### 4.1 NTP-SVM logits

The following nuclear norm-minimization problem plays a key role in our results.

###### Definition 3.

Define the NTP-SVM logit matrix 𝐋mmsuperscript𝐋mm\bm{L}^{\rm{mm}} as solution to the following optimization:

|  |  |  |  |  |

|  |  |  |  |  |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
|  |  | subj. to​𝑳​[z,j]−𝑳​[z′,j]=0,∀j∈[m],z≠z′∈𝒮j,formulae-sequencesubj. to𝑳𝑧𝑗𝑳superscript𝑧′𝑗0formulae-sequencefor-all𝑗delimited-[]𝑚𝑧superscript𝑧′subscript𝒮𝑗\displaystyle\quad~{}~{}~{}~{}\text{\emph{subj. to}}~{}~{}\bm{L}[z,j]-\bm{L}[z^{\prime},j]=0,~{}~{}\forall j\in[m],z\neq z^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{S}}\_{j}, |  | (9) |

The optimization in [NTP-SVM⋆subscriptNTP-SVM⋆\text{NTP-SVM}\_{\star}](#S4.Ex6 "In Definition 3. ‣ 4.1 NTP-SVM logits ‣ 4 Analysis of unconstrained features model for NTP training ‣ Implicit Geometry of Next-token Prediction: From Language Sparsity Patterns to Model Representations") is a semidefinite program (SDP) and always feasible, as shown by the straightforward feasibility of the centered support matrix 𝑺~=(𝕀V−1V​𝟙V​𝟙V⊤)​𝑺~𝑺subscript𝕀𝑉1𝑉subscript1𝑉superscriptsubscript1𝑉top𝑺\widetilde{\bm{S}}=(\mathds{I}\_{V}-\frac{1}{V}\mathds{1}\_{V}\mathds{1}\_{V}^{\top})\bm{S}. Its solution 𝑳mmsuperscript𝑳mm\bm{L}^{\rm{mm}} depends solely on the support matrix 𝑺𝑺\bm{S} of the data. Interestingly, we prove in Sec. [4.4](#S4.SS4 "4.4 On the solution of \"NTP-SVM\"_⋆ ‣ 4 Analysis of unconstrained features model for NTP training ‣ Implicit Geometry of Next-token Prediction: From Language Sparsity Patterns to Model Representations") that 𝑺~~𝑺\widetilde{\bm{S}} solves [NTP-SVM⋆subscriptNTP-SVM⋆\text{NTP-SVM}\_{\star}](#S4.Ex6 "In Definition 3. ‣ 4.1 NTP-SVM logits ‣ 4 Analysis of unconstrained features model for NTP training ‣ Implicit Geometry of Next-token Prediction: From Language Sparsity Patterns to Model Representations") under perfect symmetry assumptions on 𝑺𝑺\bm{S}. More generally, our empirical results suggest that 𝑺~~𝑺\widetilde{\bm{S}} closely approximates the true solution 𝑳mmsuperscript𝑳mm\bm{L}^{\rm{mm}}, supporting the use of Proxy [(P)](#S1.I3.ix1 "item (P) ‣ 1.2 Summary of findings ‣ 1 Introduction ‣ Implicit Geometry of Next-token Prediction: From Language Sparsity Patterns to Model Representations") as explained in Sec. [4.4.2](#S4.SS4.SSS2 "4.4.2 A simple candidate solution ‣ 4.4 On the solution of \"NTP-SVM\"_⋆ ‣ 4 Analysis of unconstrained features model for NTP training ‣ Implicit Geometry of Next-token Prediction: From Language Sparsity Patterns to Model Representations").

We remark that while the constraints are defined for all pairs of tokens, for each context j𝑗j, the number of linearly independent constraints imposed by Eqs. ([9](#S4.E9 "In Definition 3. ‣ 4.1 NTP-SVM logits ‣ 4 Analysis of unconstrained features model for NTP training ‣ Implicit Geometry of Next-token Prediction: From Language Sparsity Patterns to Model Representations")) and ([10](#S4.E10 "In Definition 3. ‣ 4.1 NTP-SVM logits ‣ 4 Analysis of unconstrained features model for NTP training ‣ Implicit Geometry of Next-token Prediction: From Language Sparsity Patterns to Model Representations")) is V−1𝑉1V-1. This reduction is possible because the constraints can be reformulated relative to an anchor zj∈𝒮jsubscript𝑧𝑗subscript𝒮𝑗z\_{j}\in{\mathcal{S}}\_{j} given the equality constraints ([9](#S4.E9 "In Definition 3. ‣ 4.1 NTP-SVM logits ‣ 4 Analysis of unconstrained features model for NTP training ‣ Implicit Geometry of Next-token Prediction: From Language Sparsity Patterns to Model Representations")).

Finally, when Sj=1,∀j∈[m]formulae-sequencesubscript𝑆𝑗1for-all𝑗delimited-[]𝑚S\_{j}=1,\forall j\in[m], then Eq. ([9](#S4.E9 "In Definition 3. ‣ 4.1 NTP-SVM logits ‣ 4 Analysis of unconstrained features model for NTP training ‣ Implicit Geometry of Next-token Prediction: From Language Sparsity Patterns to Model Representations")) vanishes and Eq. ([10](#S4.E10 "In Definition 3. ‣ 4.1 NTP-SVM logits ‣ 4 Analysis of unconstrained features model for NTP training ‣ Implicit Geometry of Next-token Prediction: From Language Sparsity Patterns to Model Representations")) lower bounds the margin between the correct token and the rest. This constraint appears in the classical hard-margin SVM, which explains our naming. Instead, the NTP setting results in additional equality constraints and minimizes nuclear-norm rather than Euclidean norm.

### 4.2 Regularization-path analysis of logits

We now characterize the solutions of ([8](#S3.E8 "In Lemma 1. ‣ 3.3 Reformulation in terms of logits ‣ 3 Formulation ‣ Implicit Geometry of Next-token Prediction: From Language Sparsity Patterns to Model Representations")) when regularization vanishes and d≥V𝑑𝑉d\geq V.

###### Theorem 1.

Suppose d≥V𝑑𝑉d\geq V. In the limit of vanishing regularization, the solution 𝐋λsubscript𝐋𝜆\bm{L}\_{\lambda} of optimization ([8](#S3.E8 "In Lemma 1. ‣ 3.3 Reformulation in terms of logits ‣ 3 Formulation ‣ Implicit Geometry of Next-token Prediction: From Language Sparsity Patterns to Model Representations")) diverges in norm and converges in direction to 𝐋mmsuperscript𝐋mm\bm{L}^{\rm{mm}}. Formally, limλ→0‖𝐋λ‖=+∞subscript→𝜆0normsubscript𝐋𝜆\lim\_{{\lambda}\rightarrow 0}\|\bm{L}\_{\lambda}\|=+\infty, and, there exists minimizer 𝐋mmsuperscript𝐋mm\bm{L}^{\rm{mm}} of [NTP-SVM⋆subscriptNTP-SVMitalic-⋆\text{NTP-SVM}\_{\star}](#S4.Ex6 "In Definition 3. ‣ 4.1 NTP-SVM logits ‣ 4 Analysis of unconstrained features model for NTP training ‣ Implicit Geometry of Next-token Prediction: From Language Sparsity Patterns to Model Representations") such that

|  |  |  |
| --- | --- | --- |
|  | limλ→0‖𝑳λ‖𝑳λ‖∗−𝑳mm∥𝑳mm∥∗‖=0.subscript→𝜆0normsubscript𝑳𝜆subscriptnormsubscript𝑳𝜆superscript𝑳mmsubscriptdelimited-∥∥superscript𝑳mm0\lim\_{{{\lambda}\rightarrow 0}}\,\,\big{\|}{\frac{\bm{L}\_{\lambda}}{\|\bm{L}\_{\lambda}\|\_{\*}}-\frac{\bm{L}^{\rm{mm}}}{\lVert\bm{L}^{\rm{mm}}\rVert\_{\*}}}\big{\|}=0\,. |  |

Thus, with vanishing regularization, [NTP-SVM⋆subscriptNTP-SVM⋆\text{NTP-SVM}\_{\star}](#S4.Ex6 "In Definition 3. ‣ 4.1 NTP-SVM logits ‣ 4 Analysis of unconstrained features model for NTP training ‣ Implicit Geometry of Next-token Prediction: From Language Sparsity Patterns to Model Representations") captures the structure of the logits. By Lemma [1](#Thmlemma1 "Lemma 1. ‣ 3.3 Reformulation in terms of logits ‣ 3 Formulation ‣ Implicit Geometry of Next-token Prediction: From Language Sparsity Patterns to Model Representations"), it also captures the structure of 𝑾𝑾{\bm{W}} and 𝑯𝑯{\bm{H}}. We explore this further in Sec. [4.3](#S4.SS3 "4.3 Geometry of embeddings ‣ 4 Analysis of unconstrained features model for NTP training ‣ Implicit Geometry of Next-token Prediction: From Language Sparsity Patterns to Model Representations").

Now, recall that [NTP-SVM⋆subscriptNTP-SVM⋆\text{NTP-SVM}\_{\star}](#S4.Ex6 "In Definition 3. ‣ 4.1 NTP-SVM logits ‣ 4 Analysis of unconstrained features model for NTP training ‣ Implicit Geometry of Next-token Prediction: From Language Sparsity Patterns to Model Representations") only depends on the support matrix of the training data, raising the questions: How do the probabilities p^j,zsubscript^𝑝

𝑗𝑧\hat{p}\_{j,z} influence the learned logit matrix and what implications this has for the loss? The theorem below complements Thm. [1](#Thmtheorem1 "Theorem 1. ‣ 4.2 Regularization-path analysis of logits ‣ 4 Analysis of unconstrained features model for NTP training ‣ Implicit Geometry of Next-token Prediction: From Language Sparsity Patterns to Model Representations") and addresses these questions. To state the result, define the following matrix subspace, as the span of rank-one matrices each being zero except entries (z,j)𝑧𝑗(z,j) and (z′,j)superscript𝑧′𝑗(z^{\prime},j), which equal 111 and −11-1 respectively for all context indices j∈[m]𝑗delimited-[]𝑚j\in[m] and in-support word indices z,z′∈𝒮j

This justifies Claim [(C1)](#S1.I1.ix1 "item (C1) ‣ 1.2 Summary of findings ‣ 1 Introduction ‣ Implicit Geometry of Next-token Prediction: From Language Sparsity Patterns to Model Representations").

### 4.3 Geometry of embeddings

In view of Lemma [1](#Thmlemma1 "Lemma 1. ‣ 3.3 Reformulation in terms of logits ‣ 3 Formulation ‣ Implicit Geometry of Next-token Prediction: From Language Sparsity Patterns to Model Representations") we can obtain the embeddings by factorizing the logit matrix. Since logits diverge, the same is true for the embeddings, which also converge directionally.

Denote (𝐖λ,𝐇λ)subscript𝐖𝜆subscript𝐇𝜆({\bm{W}}\_{\lambda},{\bm{H}}\_{\lambda}) any minimizer of [NTP-UFM](#S3.Ex4 "In Definition 1 (NTP-UFM). ‣ 3.2 Unconstrained features model for NTP training ‣ 3 Formulation ‣ Implicit Geometry of Next-token Prediction: From Language Sparsity Patterns to Model Representations"). Consider the SVD 𝐋mm=𝐔​𝚺​𝐕⊤superscript𝐋mm𝐔𝚺superscript𝐕top\bm{L}^{\rm{mm}}=\bm{U}\bm{\Sigma}{\bm{V}}^{\top}. Then, using ⋅¯¯⋅\overline{\,\cdot\,} notation to denote normalized quantities, in the limit of vanishing regularization

| --- | --- | --- |

This corollary supports Claim [(C2)](#S1.I2.ix1 "item (C2) ‣ 1.2 Summary of findings ‣ 1 Introduction ‣ Implicit Geometry of Next-token Prediction: From Language Sparsity Patterns to Model Representations"). Another direct consequence of it is Claim [(C3)](#S1.I2.ix2 "item (C3) ‣ 1.2 Summary of findings ‣ 1 Introduction ‣ Implicit Geometry of Next-token Prediction: From Language Sparsity Patterns to Model Representations"): embeddings of contexts whose support sets are identical asymptotically collapse to the same embedding as λ→0→𝜆0{{\lambda}\rightarrow 0}.

Assume [NTP-SVM⋆subscriptNTP-SVMitalic-⋆\text{NTP-SVM}\_{\star}](#S4.Ex6 "In Definition 3. ‣ 4.1 NTP-SVM logits ‣ 4 Analysis of unconstrained features model for NTP training ‣ Implicit Geometry of Next-token Prediction: From Language Sparsity Patterns to Model Representations") is feasible. 𝑗superscript𝑗′

This is consistent with our empirical observations in Figs. [1](#S1.F1 "Figure 1 ‣ 1 Introduction ‣ Implicit Geometry of Next-token Prediction: From Language Sparsity Patterns to Model Representations") and [2](#S1.F2 "Figure 2 ‣ 1.2 Summary of findings ‣ 1 Introduction ‣ Implicit Geometry of Next-token Prediction: From Language Sparsity Patterns to Model Representations"): As the loss approaches its lower bound, context embeddings with the same support set converge in the same direction and exhibit maximum correlation. ### 4.4 On the solution of [NTP-SVM⋆subscriptNTP-SVM⋆\text{NTP-SVM}\_{\star}](#S4.Ex6 "In Definition 3. ‣ 4.1 NTP-SVM logits ‣ 4 Analysis of unconstrained features model for NTP training ‣ Implicit Geometry of Next-token Prediction: From Language Sparsity Patterns to Model Representations")

To better understand how the SVD factorization informs the geometry, we investigate further how the structure of 𝑳mmsuperscript𝑳mm\bm{L}^{\rm{mm}} (solution to [NTP-SVM⋆subscriptNTP-SVM⋆\text{NTP-SVM}\_{\star}](#S4.Ex6 "In Definition 3. ‣ 4.1 NTP-SVM logits ‣ 4 Analysis of unconstrained features model for NTP training ‣ Implicit Geometry of Next-token Prediction: From Language Sparsity Patterns to Model Representations")) depends on the support matrix 𝑺𝑺\bm{S}.

We start in Sec. [4.4.1](#S4.SS4.SSS1 "4.4.1 Special case: support sets of equal sizes ‣ 4.4 On the solution of \"NTP-SVM\"_⋆ ‣ 4 Analysis of unconstrained features model for NTP training ‣ Implicit Geometry of Next-token Prediction: From Language Sparsity Patterns to Model Representations") with an idealized, perfectly symmetric setting where each of the
(Vk)binomial𝑉𝑘V\choose k contexts is followed by a unique set of
k𝑘k words. While this configuration is admittedly too symmetric to closely mimic natural language, it fulfills two functions. First, it allows for explicit calculation of the implicit geometry—specifically, the angles and norms of embeddings. Second, and more importantly for our analysis, it serves as a basis in Sec. [4.4.2](#S4.SS4.SSS2 "4.4.2 A simple candidate solution ‣ 4.4 On the solution of \"NTP-SVM\"_⋆ ‣ 4 Analysis of unconstrained features model for NTP training ‣ Implicit Geometry of Next-token Prediction: From Language Sparsity Patterns to Model Representations") for approximating 𝑳mmsuperscript𝑳mm\bm{L}^{\rm{mm}} in more general scenarios, thereby motivating the heuristic proxy [(P)](#S1.I3.ix1 "item (P) ‣ 1.2 Summary of findings ‣ 1 Introduction ‣ Implicit Geometry of Next-token Prediction: From Language Sparsity Patterns to Model Representations") in Sec. [4.4.3](#S4.SS4.SSS3 "4.4.3 A proxy for \"NTP-SVM\"_⋆ solutions ‣ 4.4 On the solution of \"NTP-SVM\"_⋆ ‣ 4 Analysis of unconstrained features model for NTP training ‣ Implicit Geometry of Next-token Prediction: From Language Sparsity Patterns to Model Representations").

#### 4.4.1 Special case: support sets of equal sizes

###### Proposition 2.

Fix k∈[V−1]𝑘delimited-[]𝑉1k\in[V-1] and suppose 𝐒𝐒\bm{S} contains all m=(Vk)𝑚binomial𝑉𝑘m={V\choose k} support sets of size k𝑘k. Then,

1. (i)

   The logit matrix takes the form 𝑳mm=(𝕀V−1V​𝟙𝟙⊤)​𝑺≜𝑺~superscript𝑳mmsubscript𝕀𝑉1𝑉superscript11top𝑺≜~𝑺\bm{L}^{\rm{mm}}=(\mathds{I}\_{V}-\frac{1}{V}\mathds{1}\mathds{1}^{\top})\bm{S}\triangleq\widetilde{\bm{S}}.
2. (ii)

   Word embeddings form equiangular tight frame (ETF) being equinorm and maximally separated.
3. (iii)

   Context embeddings are equinorm and the embedding 𝒉jsubscript𝒉𝑗\bm{h}\_{j} of context j𝑗j is co-linear to ∑z∈𝒮j𝒘zsubscript𝑧subscript𝒮𝑗subscript𝒘𝑧\sum\_{z\in{\mathcal{S}}\_{j}}{\bm{w}}\_{z}.

In the symmetric setting of the proposition, we can analytically solve [NTP-SVM⋆subscriptNTP-SVM⋆\text{NTP-SVM}\_{\star}](#S4.Ex6 "In Definition 3. ‣ 4.1 NTP-SVM logits ‣ 4 Analysis of unconstrained features model for NTP training ‣ Implicit Geometry of Next-token Prediction: From Language Sparsity Patterns to Model Representations") giving 𝑳mmsuperscript𝑳mm\bm{L}^{\rm{mm}} as expressed in the statement (i). In fact, it is possible to obtain an explicit characterization of the SVD of 𝑳mmsuperscript𝑳mm\bm{L}^{\rm{mm}}, which enables the precise definition of embeddings’ geometries in statements (ii) and (iii); see also Prop. [5](#Thmproposition5 "Proposition 5. ‣ C.1 Special case: support sets of equal sizes ‣ Appendix C Additional discussions on the solution of \"NTP-SVM\"_⋆ ‣ Implicit Geometry of Next-token Prediction: From Language Sparsity Patterns to Model Representations") in the appendix.
For k=1𝑘1k=1 the embeddings recover the ETF geometry that has been previously shown for the setting of one-hot classification (Papyan et al., [2020](#bib.bib46)). For general k>1𝑘1k>1, word embeddings continue forming an ETF, but the geometry of context embeddings changes although they remain equinorm. Embedding 𝒉jsubscript𝒉𝑗\bm{h}\_{j} forms the same *acute* angle with all in-support word vectors 𝒘v,v∈𝒮j

subscript𝒘𝑣𝑣
subscript𝒮𝑗{\bm{w}}\_{v},v\in{\mathcal{S}}\_{j} and the same *obtuse* angle with all out-of-support word vectors 𝒘v,v∉𝒮j

subscript𝒘𝑣𝑣
subscript𝒮𝑗{\bm{w}}\_{v},v\notin{\mathcal{S}}\_{j}. Additionally, for k>1𝑘1k>1, norms of word embedddings become larger than norms of context embeddings. See also Fig. [8](#A3.F8 "Figure 8 ‣ C.1 Special case: support sets of equal sizes ‣ Appendix C Additional discussions on the solution of \"NTP-SVM\"_⋆ ‣ Implicit Geometry of Next-token Prediction: From Language Sparsity Patterns to Model Representations") for a visualization of these properties.

#### 4.4.2 A simple candidate solution

Achieving an analytic expression for 𝑳mmsuperscript𝑳mm\bm{L}^{\rm{mm}}, as was possible in Prop. [2](#Thmproposition2 "Proposition 2. ‣ 4.4.1 Special case: support sets of equal sizes ‣ 4.4 On the solution of \"NTP-SVM\"_⋆ ‣ 4 Analysis of unconstrained features model for NTP training ‣ Implicit Geometry of Next-token Prediction: From Language Sparsity Patterns to Model Representations"), might not always be possible. This challenge primarily arises from the combinatorial complexity of the constraints that adhere to the sparsity pattern of 𝑺𝑺\bm{S}. To mitigate this issue and potentially circumvent the need to solve the semidefinite program in [NTP-SVM⋆subscriptNTP-SVM⋆\text{NTP-SVM}\_{\star}](#S4.Ex6 "In Definition 3. ‣ 4.1 NTP-SVM logits ‣ 4 Analysis of unconstrained features model for NTP training ‣ Implicit Geometry of Next-token Prediction: From Language Sparsity Patterns to Model Representations")—which becomes computationally intensive for large V𝑉V and m𝑚m—we introduce a strategy that allows for a numerical verification of whether 𝑺~~𝑺\widetilde{\bm{S}} of Prop. [2](#Thmproposition2 "Proposition 2. ‣ 4.4.1 Special case: support sets of equal sizes ‣ 4.4 On the solution of \"NTP-SVM\"_⋆ ‣ 4 Analysis of unconstrained features model for NTP training ‣ Implicit Geometry of Next-token Prediction: From Language Sparsity Patterns to Model Representations") solves [NTP-SVM⋆subscriptNTP-SVM⋆\text{NTP-SVM}\_{\star}](#S4.Ex6 "In Definition 3. ‣ 4.1 NTP-SVM logits ‣ 4 Analysis of unconstrained features model for NTP training ‣ Implicit Geometry of Next-token Prediction: From Language Sparsity Patterns to Model Representations").
Note that 𝑺~~𝑺\widetilde{\bm{S}} readily satisfies the feasibility conditions for [NTP-SVM⋆subscriptNTP-SVM⋆\text{NTP-SVM}\_{\star}](#S4.Ex6 "In Definition 3. ‣ 4.1 NTP-SVM logits ‣ 4 Analysis of unconstrained features model for NTP training ‣ Implicit Geometry of Next-token Prediction: From Language Sparsity Patterns to Model Representations"), meeting all inequality constraints with equality. Below, we further introduce a dual-certificate condition that, when fulfilled by 𝑺~~𝑺\widetilde{\bm{S}}, ensures optimality.

###### Proposition 3.

Let 𝐒~=𝐔​𝚺​𝐕⊤~𝐒𝐔𝚺superscript𝐕top\widetilde{\bm{S}}=\bm{U}\bm{\Sigma}{\bm{V}}^{\top} denote the SVD of 𝐒~=(𝕀V−1V​𝟙𝟙⊤)​𝐒~𝐒subscript𝕀𝑉1𝑉superscript11top𝐒\widetilde{\bm{S}}=(\mathds{I}\_{V}-\frac{1}{V}\mathds{1}\mathds{1}^{\top})\bm{S}. Define, 𝐀:=𝐔​𝐕⊤assign𝐀𝐔superscript𝐕top{\bm{A}}:=\bm{U}{\bm{V}}^{\top}. If ∀j∈[m]for-all𝑗delimited-[]𝑚\forall j\in[m] and all v∉𝒮j𝑣subscript𝒮𝑗v\not\in{\mathcal{S}}\_{j}, it holds that 𝐀​[v,j]<0𝐀𝑣𝑗0{\bm{A}}[v,j]<0, then 𝐒~~𝐒\widetilde{\bm{S}} solves [NTP-SVM⋆subscriptNTP-SVMitalic-⋆\text{NTP-SVM}\_{\star}](#S4.Ex6 "In Definition 3. ‣ 4.1 NTP-SVM logits ‣ 4 Analysis of unconstrained features model for NTP training ‣ Implicit Geometry of Next-token Prediction: From Language Sparsity Patterns to Model Representations").

Verifying the certificate’s conditions only requires an SVD of 𝑺~~𝑺\widetilde{\bm{S}}. In situations where these SVD factors can be analytically determined, the certificate also enables a formal proof of optimality (e.g., Prop. [2](#Thmproposition2 "Proposition 2. ‣ 4.4.1 Special case: support sets of equal sizes ‣ 4.4 On the solution of \"NTP-SVM\"_⋆ ‣ 4 Analysis of unconstrained features model for NTP training ‣ Implicit Geometry of Next-token Prediction: From Language Sparsity Patterns to Model Representations")).

In more complex settings than that of Prop. [2](#Thmproposition2 "Proposition 2. ‣ 4.4.1 Special case: support sets of equal sizes ‣ 4.4 On the solution of \"NTP-SVM\"_⋆ ‣ 4 Analysis of unconstrained features model for NTP training ‣ Implicit Geometry of Next-token Prediction: From Language Sparsity Patterns to Model Representations"), whether 𝑺~~𝑺\widetilde{\bm{S}} satisfies the dual certificate in Prop. [3](#Thmproposition3 "Proposition 3. ‣ 4.4.2 A simple candidate solution ‣ 4.4 On the solution of \"NTP-SVM\"_⋆ ‣ 4 Analysis of unconstrained features model for NTP training ‣ Implicit Geometry of Next-token Prediction: From Language Sparsity Patterns to Model Representations") or not, and thus its optimality, depends on the sparsity pattern of 𝑺𝑺\bm{S}. App. [C.2](#A3.SS2 "C.2 A simple candidate solution ‣ Appendix C Additional discussions on the solution of \"NTP-SVM\"_⋆ ‣ Implicit Geometry of Next-token Prediction: From Language Sparsity Patterns to Model Representations") compares 𝑺~~𝑺\widetilde{\bm{S}} to the optimal solution on a few small-scale examples. Although not necessarily optimal for all configurations of the support set, we conjecture that 𝑺~~𝑺\widetilde{\bm{S}}, gives an approximation with almost similar geometric properties of the optimal solution 𝑳mmsuperscript𝑳mm\bm{L}^{\rm{mm}}. This conjecture motivates a more efficient proxy for predicting the implicit geometry in the next section.

#### 4.4.3 A proxy for [NTP-SVM⋆subscriptNTP-SVM⋆\text{NTP-SVM}\_{\star}](#S4.Ex6 "In Definition 3. ‣ 4.1 NTP-SVM logits ‣ 4 Analysis of unconstrained features model for NTP training ‣ Implicit Geometry of Next-token Prediction: From Language Sparsity Patterns to Model Representations") solutions

Suppose 𝑳mm≈𝑺~superscript𝑳mm~𝑺\bm{L}^{\rm{mm}}\approx\widetilde{\bm{S}}. |  | 𝑾mm​𝑾mm⊤superscript𝑾mmsuperscriptsuperscript𝑾mmtop\displaystyle{\bm{W}}^{\rm{mm}}{{\bm{W}}^{\rm{mm}}}^{\top} | =(𝑳mm​𝑳mm⊤)12≈(𝑺~​𝑺~⊤)12,absentsuperscriptsuperscript𝑳mmsuperscriptsuperscript𝑳mmtop12superscript~𝑺superscript~𝑺top12\displaystyle=(\bm{L}^{\rm{mm}}{\bm{L}^{\rm{mm}}}^{\top})^{\frac{1}{2}}\approx(\widetilde{\bm{S}}{\widetilde{\bm{S}}}^{\top})^{\frac{1}{2}}, |  |

| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
|  | 𝑯mm⊤​𝑯mmsuperscriptsuperscript𝑯mmtopsuperscript𝑯mm\displaystyle{{\bm{H}}^{\rm{mm}}}^{\top}{\bm{H}}^{\rm{mm}} | =(𝑳mm⊤​𝑳mm)12≈(𝑺~⊤​𝑺~)12.absentsuperscriptsuperscriptsuperscript𝑳mmtopsuperscript𝑳mm12superscriptsuperscript~𝑺top~𝑺12\displaystyle=({\bm{L}^{\rm{mm}}}^{\top}\bm{L}^{\rm{mm}})^{\frac{1}{2}}\approx({\widetilde{\bm{S}}}^{\top}\widetilde{\bm{S}})^{\frac{1}{2}}. |  | (11) |

Computing the matrix square roots in Eq. ([4.4.3](#S4.Ex11 "4.4.3 A proxy for \"NTP-SVM\"_⋆ solutions ‣ 4.4 On the solution of \"NTP-SVM\"_⋆ ‣ 4 Analysis of unconstrained features model for NTP training ‣ Implicit Geometry of Next-token Prediction: From Language Sparsity Patterns to Model Representations")) is as expensive as an SVD decomposition which can be prohibitive for large matrices. Instead, we suggest the following more computationally efficient proxies for estimating the directional component of the implicit geometries:

|  |  |  |  |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
|  | 𝑾mm​𝑾mm⊤superscript𝑾mmsuperscriptsuperscript𝑾mmtop\displaystyle{\bm{W}}^{\rm{mm}}{{\bm{W}}^{\rm{mm}}}^{\top} | ≈𝑺~​𝑺~⊤=(𝕀V−1V​𝟙V​𝟙V⊤)​𝑺​𝑺⊤​(𝕀V−1V​𝟙V​𝟙V⊤),absent~𝑺superscript~𝑺topsubscript𝕀𝑉1𝑉subscript1𝑉superscriptsubscript1𝑉top𝑺superscript𝑺topsubscript𝕀𝑉1𝑉subscript1𝑉superscriptsubscript1𝑉top\displaystyle\approx\widetilde{\bm{S}}\widetilde{\bm{S}}^{\top}=(\mathds{I}\_{V}-\frac{1}{V}\mathds{1}\_{V}\mathds{1}\_{V}^{\top})\bm{S}\bm{S}^{\top}(\mathds{I}\_{V}-\frac{1}{V}\mathds{1}\_{V}\mathds{1}\_{V}^{\top}), |  |
|  |  |  |  |  |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
|  | 𝑯mm⊤​𝑯mmsuperscriptsuperscript𝑯mmtopsuperscript𝑯mm\displaystyle{{\bm{H}}^{\rm{mm}}}^{\top}{\bm{H}}^{\rm{mm}} | ≈𝑺~⊤​𝑺~=𝑺⊤​(𝕀V−1V​𝟙V​𝟙V⊤)​𝑺,absentsuperscript~𝑺top~𝑺superscript𝑺topsubscript𝕀𝑉1𝑉subscript1𝑉superscriptsubscript1𝑉top𝑺\displaystyle\approx\widetilde{\bm{S}}^{\top}\widetilde{\bm{S}}=\bm{S}^{\top}(\mathds{I}\_{V}-\frac{1}{V}\mathds{1}\_{V}\mathds{1}\_{V}^{\top})\bm{S}, |  | (12) |

This leads to Proxy [(P)](#S1.I3.ix1 "item (P) ‣ 1.2 Summary of findings ‣ 1 Introduction ‣ Implicit Geometry of Next-token Prediction: From Language Sparsity Patterns to Model Representations") introduced in Sec. [1.2](#S1.SS2 "1.2 Summary of findings ‣ 1 Introduction ‣ Implicit Geometry of Next-token Prediction: From Language Sparsity Patterns to Model Representations").

Ignoring for simplicity the projection to the 𝕀V−1V​𝟙V​𝟙V⊤subscript𝕀𝑉1𝑉subscript1𝑉superscriptsubscript1𝑉top\mathds{I}\_{V}-\frac{1}{V}\mathds{1}\_{V}\mathds{1}\_{V}^{\top} subspace, Eq. ([4.4.3](#S4.Ex12 "4.4.3 A proxy for \"NTP-SVM\"_⋆ solutions ‣ 4.4 On the solution of \"NTP-SVM\"_⋆ ‣ 4 Analysis of unconstrained features model for NTP training ‣ Implicit Geometry of Next-token Prediction: From Language Sparsity Patterns to Model Representations")) gives a simple explanation for similarity between embeddings: For given context j∈[m]𝑗delimited-[]𝑚j\in[m], the corresponding embedding has higher correlation with contexts j′superscript𝑗′j^{\prime} whose support set intersection |𝒮j∩𝒮j′|subscript𝒮𝑗subscript𝒮superscript𝑗′|{\mathcal{S}}\_{j}\cap{\mathcal{S}}\_{j^{\prime}}| is larger. Similarly, word embeddings are closer if the respective words appear together in more support sets: word embedding 𝒘zsubscript𝒘𝑧{\bm{w}}\_{z} is more similar to 𝒘z′subscript𝒘superscript𝑧′{\bm{w}}\_{z^{\prime}} when |{j:𝒘z,𝒘z′∈𝒮j,j∈[m]}|conditional-set𝑗formulae-sequence

subscript𝒘𝑧subscript𝒘superscript𝑧′
subscript𝒮𝑗𝑗delimited-[]𝑚|\{j\,:\,{\bm{w}}\_{z},{\bm{w}}\_{z^{\prime}}\in{\mathcal{S}}\_{j},\,j\in[m]\}| is larger. The projection then fixes the bias of the parameters: since ridge-regularization is strongly convex and softmax is invariant to shift, any minimizer of [NTP-UFM](#S3.Ex4 "In Definition 1 (NTP-UFM). ‣ 3.2 Unconstrained features model for NTP training ‣ 3 Formulation ‣ Implicit Geometry of Next-token Prediction: From Language Sparsity Patterns to Model Representations") satisfies 𝟙V⊤​𝑾=0superscriptsubscript1𝑉top𝑾0\mathds{1}\_{V}^{\top}{\bm{W}}=0 ( see Lemma [2](#Thmlemma2 "Lemma 2. ‣ B.1 Centering of word embeddings ‣ Appendix B Proofs ‣ Implicit Geometry of Next-token Prediction: From Language Sparsity Patterns to Model Representations")).

Through numerical experiments training on purely synthetic data, we confirm that the solution derived from (stochastic) gradient descent optimization of [NTP-UFM](#S3.Ex4 "In Definition 1 (NTP-UFM). ‣ 3.2 Unconstrained features model for NTP training ‣ 3 Formulation ‣ Implicit Geometry of Next-token Prediction: From Language Sparsity Patterns to Model Representations") is consistent with the analysis presented in Sec. [4](#S4 "4 Analysis of unconstrained features model for NTP training ‣ Implicit Geometry of Next-token Prediction: From Language Sparsity Patterns to Model Representations").

Specifically, we choose vocabulary size V=10𝑉10V=10, embedding dimension d=10𝑑10d=10, and m=95𝑚95m=95 training samples and generate (centered) support matrix 𝑺~~𝑺\widetilde{\bm{S}} as shown in Fig. [4](#S4.F4 "Figure 4 ‣ 4.5 Numerical simulation: Verifying the theory ‣ 4 Analysis of unconstrained features model for NTP training ‣ Implicit Geometry of Next-token Prediction: From Language Sparsity Patterns to Model Representations")-(c). We also set π^j=1/msubscript^𝜋𝑗1𝑚\hat{\pi}\_{j}={1}/{m} and for this fixed support matrix 𝑺𝑺\bm{S}, we generate random soft labels 𝒑^j∈ΔV−1subscript^𝒑𝑗superscriptΔ𝑉1\hat{\bm{p}}\_{j}\in\Delta^{V-1} such that p^j,v=0,v∉𝒮jformulae-sequencesubscript^𝑝

𝑗𝑣0𝑣subscript𝒮𝑗\hat{p}\_{j,v}=0,v\not\in{\mathcal{S}}\_{j}.

We display the training evolution in Fig. [4](#S4.F4 "Figure 4 ‣ 4.5 Numerical simulation: Verifying the theory ‣ 4 Analysis of unconstrained features model for NTP training ‣ Implicit Geometry of Next-token Prediction: From Language Sparsity Patterns to Model Representations"). We train [NTP-UFM](#S3.Ex4 "In Definition 1 (NTP-UFM). ‣ 3.2 Unconstrained features model for NTP training ‣ 3 Formulation ‣ Implicit Geometry of Next-token Prediction: From Language Sparsity Patterns to Model Representations") with SGD and small weight decay λ=10−5𝜆superscript105\lambda=10^{-5} until reaching the empirical entropy lower-bound ℋ≈1.04ℋ1.04\mathcal{H}\approx 1.04 within an order of 10−4superscript10410^{-4} as shown in Fig. [4](#S4.F4 "Figure 4 ‣ 4.5 Numerical simulation: Verifying the theory ‣ 4 Analysis of unconstrained features model for NTP training ‣ Implicit Geometry of Next-token Prediction: From Language Sparsity Patterns to Model Representations")-(a). This decrease in the loss is accompanied by a consistent increase in the parameter norms, as displayed in Panel (b). Despite the increase in norms, the projection of 𝑳ksubscript𝑳𝑘\bm{L}\_{k} onto the subspace ℱℱ\mathcal{F} remains close to the finite component 𝑳insuperscript𝑳in\bm{L}^{\rm{in}} specified by Thm. [2](#Thmtheorem2 "Theorem 2. ‣ 4.2 Regularization-path analysis of logits ‣ 4 Analysis of unconstrained features model for NTP training ‣ Implicit Geometry of Next-token Prediction: From Language Sparsity Patterns to Model Representations"). This is illustrated in Panel (c) and ensures the recovery of the soft-labels on the in-support tokens.

In Fig. [4](#S4.F4 "Figure 4 ‣ 4.5 Numerical simulation: Verifying the theory ‣ 4 Analysis of unconstrained features model for NTP training ‣ Implicit Geometry of Next-token Prediction: From Language Sparsity Patterns to Model Representations"), we visualize the logits 𝑳k=𝑾k​𝑯ksubscript𝑳𝑘subscript𝑾𝑘subscript𝑯𝑘\bm{L}\_{k}={\bm{W}}\_{k}{\bm{H}}\_{k}, and cosine similarities corr​(𝑾k⊤)corrsuperscriptsubscript𝑾𝑘top\textsc{corr}({\bm{W}}\_{k}^{\top}) and corr​(𝑯k)corrsubscript𝑯𝑘\textsc{corr}({\bm{H}}\_{k}) of word and context embeddings, respectively.
As shown in Panel (a) of Fig. [4](#S4.F4 "Figure 4 ‣ 4.5 Numerical simulation: Verifying the theory ‣ 4 Analysis of unconstrained features model for NTP training ‣ Implicit Geometry of Next-token Prediction: From Language Sparsity Patterns to Model Representations"), as training continues the parameters recover the implicit geometry in Panel (b), which visualizes the prediction made by Thm. [1](#Thmtheorem1 "Theorem 1. ‣ 4.2 Regularization-path analysis of logits ‣ 4 Analysis of unconstrained features model for NTP training ‣ Implicit Geometry of Next-token Prediction: From Language Sparsity Patterns to Model Representations") and Cor. [1](#Thmcorollary1 "Corollary 1. ‣ 4.3 Geometry of embeddings ‣ 4 Analysis of unconstrained features model for NTP training ‣ Implicit Geometry of Next-token Prediction: From Language Sparsity Patterns to Model Representations"). In Panel (c), we also visualize the cosine similarity of 𝑺~~𝑺\widetilde{\bm{S}} and 𝑺~⊤superscript~𝑺top\widetilde{\bm{S}}^{\top}, which we introduced as a less expensive proxy of 𝑾mmsuperscript𝑾mm{\bm{W}}^{\rm{mm}} and 𝑯mmsuperscript𝑯mm{\bm{H}}^{\rm{mm}} (see Sec. [4.4.3](#S4.SS4.SSS3 "4.4.3 A proxy for \"NTP-SVM\"_⋆ solutions ‣ 4.4 On the solution of \"NTP-SVM\"_⋆ ‣ 4 Analysis of unconstrained features model for NTP training ‣ Implicit Geometry of Next-token Prediction: From Language Sparsity Patterns to Model Representations")). While the theory and proxy are not exactly the same, their heatmaps display similar structure. To verify the close relationship between the embeddings and the proxies quantitatively, we measure sim​(𝑯,𝑺~)sim𝑯~𝑺\textsc{sim}\big{(}{{\bm{H}}},{\widetilde{\bm{S}}}\big{)} and sim​(𝑾⊤,𝑺~⊤)simsuperscript𝑾topsuperscript~𝑺top\textsc{sim}\big{(}{{\bm{W}}^{\top}},{\widetilde{\bm{S}}^{\top}}\big{)}, where sim​(𝑿,𝐘)sim𝑿𝐘\textsc{sim}\big{(}{\bm{X}},\mathbf{Y}\big{)} measures the structural correlation between the two matrices (see Sec. [5.3](#S5.SS3 "5.3 Metrics. ‣ 5 Experiments ‣ Implicit Geometry of Next-token Prediction: From Language Sparsity Patterns to Model Representations")). Fig. [4](#S4.F4 "Figure 4 ‣ 4.5 Numerical simulation: Verifying the theory ‣ 4 Analysis of unconstrained features model for NTP training ‣ Implicit Geometry of Next-token Prediction: From Language Sparsity Patterns to Model Representations")-(d) confirms the high correlation between the proxy [(P)](#S1.I3.ix1 "item (P) ‣ 1.2 Summary of findings ‣ 1 Introduction ‣ Implicit Geometry of Next-token Prediction: From Language Sparsity Patterns to Model Representations") and the embeddings’ implicit geometry.
We also note that in the special case where the support sets of two contexts are identical, the heatmaps also verify the subspace collapse Claim [(C3)](#S1.I2.ix2 "item (C3) ‣ 1.2 Summary of findings ‣ 1 Introduction ‣ Implicit Geometry of Next-token Prediction: From Language Sparsity Patterns to Model Representations"): Along the diagonal blocks of corr​(𝑯)corr𝑯\textsc{corr}({\bm{H}}), where the contexts with similar support sets lie, the samples have maximum correlation and are aligned.

We finally note that in this experiment 𝑳mm=𝑺~superscript𝑳mm~𝑺\bm{L}^{\rm{mm}}=\widetilde{\bm{S}} (we verified that the dual-certificate condition in Prop. [3](#Thmproposition3 "Proposition 3. ‣ 4.4.2 A simple candidate solution ‣ 4.4 On the solution of \"NTP-SVM\"_⋆ ‣ 4 Analysis of unconstrained features model for NTP training ‣ Implicit Geometry of Next-token Prediction: From Language Sparsity Patterns to Model Representations") holds).

## 5 Experiments

We now empirically validate our analysis on text data.
We start with two small-scale synthetic datasets to examine Claims [(C1)](#S1.I1.ix1 "item (C1) ‣ 1.2 Summary of findings ‣ 1 Introduction ‣ Implicit Geometry of Next-token Prediction: From Language Sparsity Patterns to Model Representations")-[(C4)](#S1.I2.ix3 "item (C4) ‣ 1.2 Summary of findings ‣ 1 Introduction ‣ Implicit Geometry of Next-token Prediction: From Language Sparsity Patterns to Model Representations").
We then experiment with a larger-scale dataset, where SVD calculation are computationaly expensive, we examine the Proxy [(P)](#S1.I3.ix1 "item (P) ‣ 1.2 Summary of findings ‣ 1 Introduction ‣ Implicit Geometry of Next-token Prediction: From Language Sparsity Patterns to Model Representations").

### 5.1 Datasets

For a detailed description of the datasets, refer to App. [D.1](#A4.SS1 "D.1 Additional details and results: Sec. 5 ‣ Appendix D Numerical experiments ‣ Implicit Geometry of Next-token Prediction: From Language Sparsity Patterns to Model Representations").

Synthetic. We manually create simple (context, next-token) pairs, with context length of size T−1=5𝑇15T-1=5. The dataset consists of n=116𝑛116n=116 samples, containing m=16𝑚16m=16 distinct contexts, and a vocabulary size of V=30𝑉30V=30. Each context has a fixed support set length of Sj=3subscript𝑆𝑗3S\_{j}=3 and the empirical entropy lower-bound is ℋ=1.6597ℋ1.6597\mathcal{H}=1.6597.

Simplified TinyStories. For a more realistic but still controlled setup, we curate the dataset from the TinyStories corpus:
We derive contexts 𝒙¯jsubscript¯𝒙𝑗\bar{\bm{x}}\_{j} and support sets 𝒮jsubscript𝒮𝑗{\mathcal{S}}\_{j} by choosing the most frequent word-level contexts with length T−1=5𝑇15T-1=5. Here, V=104𝑉104V=104, n≈3050𝑛3050n\approx 3050 and m≈400𝑚400m\approx 400 with the empirical entropy lower-bound ℋ=1.0166ℋ1.0166\mathcal{H}=1.0166.

TinyStories. For more standard data, we use 100 stories sampled from TinyStories. Unlike the other two datasets, we do not sample over the contexts and support sets. We use a fixed context length T−1=6𝑇16T-1=6 for training. In the final dataset, V=128𝑉128V=128, m∼105similar-to𝑚superscript105m\sim 10^{5}, and the empirical entropy lower-bound is ℋ=0.3112ℋ0.3112\mathcal{H}=0.3112. We choose a small context window and vocabulary size to make tracking the distinct contexts and their support sets computationally manageable.

### 5.2 Models

We train decoder-only TF with 4 layers and d=128𝑑128d=128 for Synthetic and Simplified TinyStories, and 12 layers and d=256𝑑256d=256 for TinyStories. For training details, see App. [D.1](#A4.SS1 "D.1 Additional details and results: Sec. 5 ‣ Appendix D Numerical experiments ‣ Implicit Geometry of Next-token Prediction: From Language Sparsity Patterns to Model Representations").

We verify two claims made in Sec. [4](#S4 "4 Analysis of unconstrained features model for NTP training ‣ Implicit Geometry of Next-token Prediction: From Language Sparsity Patterns to Model Representations"): 1) We assess whether numerically optimizing both the TF and [NTP-UFM](#S3.Ex4 "In Definition 1 (NTP-UFM). ‣ 3.2 Unconstrained features model for NTP training ‣ 3 Formulation ‣ Implicit Geometry of Next-token Prediction: From Language Sparsity Patterns to Model Representations") in each setup yields the same embedding geometries, 2) We evaluate the global solution of [NTP-UFM](#S3.Ex4 "In Definition 1 (NTP-UFM). ‣ 3.2 Unconstrained features model for NTP training ‣ 3 Formulation ‣ Implicit Geometry of Next-token Prediction: From Language Sparsity Patterns to Model Representations"), as specified by Thm. [1](#Thmtheorem1 "Theorem 1. ‣ 4.2 Regularization-path analysis of logits ‣ 4 Analysis of unconstrained features model for NTP training ‣ Implicit Geometry of Next-token Prediction: From Language Sparsity Patterns to Model Representations") and Cor. [1](#Thmcorollary1 "Corollary 1. ‣ 4.3 Geometry of embeddings ‣ 4 Analysis of unconstrained features model for NTP training ‣ Implicit Geometry of Next-token Prediction: From Language Sparsity Patterns to Model Representations"), and check whether it respects the same implicit geometry as the TF and the [NTP-UFM](#S3.Ex4 "In Definition 1 (NTP-UFM). ‣ 3.2 Unconstrained features model for NTP training ‣ 3 Formulation ‣ Implicit Geometry of Next-token Prediction: From Language Sparsity Patterns to Model Representations").
For the first one, we train [NTP-UFM](#S3.Ex4 "In Definition 1 (NTP-UFM). ‣ 3.2 Unconstrained features model for NTP training ‣ 3 Formulation ‣ Implicit Geometry of Next-token Prediction: From Language Sparsity Patterns to Model Representations") directly on the label distribution of each dataset; this is computationally feasible only for the first two datasets. For the second claim, we find the global solution 𝑳mmsuperscript𝑳mm\bm{L}^{\rm{mm}} of [NTP-SVM⋆subscriptNTP-SVM⋆\text{NTP-SVM}\_{\star}](#S4.Ex6 "In Definition 3. ‣ 4.1 NTP-SVM logits ‣ 4 Analysis of unconstrained features model for NTP training ‣ Implicit Geometry of Next-token Prediction: From Language Sparsity Patterns to Model Representations") on each dataset using CVXPY (Diamond & Boyd, [2016](#bib.bib11)) and 𝑯mmsuperscript𝑯mm{\bm{H}}^{\rm{mm}}, 𝑾mmsuperscript𝑾mm{\bm{W}}^{\rm{mm}} using Cor. [1](#Thmcorollary1 "Corollary 1. ‣ 4.3 Geometry of embeddings ‣ 4 Analysis of unconstrained features model for NTP training ‣ Implicit Geometry of Next-token Prediction: From Language Sparsity Patterns to Model Representations"). If not computationally possible, we only use the Proxy [(P)](#S1.I3.ix1 "item (P) ‣ 1.2 Summary of findings ‣ 1 Introduction ‣ Implicit Geometry of Next-token Prediction: From Language Sparsity Patterns to Model Representations") as reference.

### 5.3 Metrics.

We define the following metrics for verifying our theoretical results:

Visualization of embeddings’ geometry. To visualize the geometry of the word and context embeddings, we plot the heatmap of the normalized Gram matrix corr​(𝑾⊤)corrsuperscript𝑾top\textsc{corr}({\bm{W}}^{\top}) and corr​(𝑯)corr𝑯\textsc{corr}({\bm{H}}), where for matrix 𝑿𝑿{\bm{X}}, corr​(𝑿)corr𝑿\textsc{corr}({\bm{X}}) is a matrix whose (i,j)𝑖𝑗(i,j)-th entry is the cosine similarity between the respective columns of 𝑿𝑿{\bm{X}}, i.e.,

|  |  |  |
| --- | --- | --- |
|  | [corr​(𝑿)]i,j=𝒙i⊤​𝒙j/(‖𝒙i‖​‖𝒙j‖).subscriptdelimited-[]corr𝑿  𝑖𝑗superscriptsubscript𝒙𝑖topsubscript𝒙𝑗normsubscript𝒙𝑖normsubscript𝒙𝑗\big{[}\textsc{corr}({\bm{X}})\big{]}\_{i,j}={\bm{x}\_{i}^{\top}\bm{x}\_{j}}/{(\|\bm{x}\_{i}\|\|\bm{x}\_{j}\|)}. |  |

To check Proxy [(P)](#S1.I3.ix1 "item (P) ‣ 1.2 Summary of findings ‣ 1 Introduction ‣ Implicit Geometry of Next-token Prediction: From Language Sparsity Patterns to Model Representations"), we compare the context and word embeddings with corr​(𝑺~)corr~𝑺\textsc{corr}(\widetilde{\bm{S}}) and corr​(𝑺~⊤)corrsuperscript~𝑺top\textsc{corr}(\widetilde{\bm{S}}^{\top}).

Quantifying geometric similarity. To measure the structural similarity between 𝑾𝑾{\bm{W}}, 𝑯𝑯{\bm{H}} and 𝑺~⊤superscript~𝑺top\widetilde{\bm{S}}^{\top}, 𝑺~~𝑺\widetilde{\bm{S}}, we use

as the correlation matrix between two matrices.555This is the Structural Similarity Index Measure (SSIM) used to measure the similarity between two images (Wang et al., [2004](#bib.bib67)).
Here, σ𝑿​𝐘subscript𝜎𝑿𝐘\sigma\_{{\bm{X}}\mathbf{Y}} is the covariance, and σ𝑿subscript𝜎𝑿\sigma\_{\bm{X}} and σ𝐘subscript𝜎𝐘\sigma\_{\mathbf{Y}} are the standard deviations of 𝑿𝑿{\bm{X}} and 𝐘𝐘\mathbf{Y}, respectively, and ϵitalic-ϵ\epsilon is a small constant for stable division. A value of sim​(𝑿,𝐘)=1sim𝑿𝐘1\textsc{sim}\big{(}{\bm{X}},\mathbf{Y}\big{)}=1 indicates perfect structural correlation.

### 5.4 Results

In Sec. [1.1](#S1.SS1 "1.1 Methodology ‣ 1 Introduction ‣ Implicit Geometry of Next-token Prediction: From Language Sparsity Patterns to Model Representations"), we discussed Fig. [1](#S1.F1 "Figure 1 ‣ 1 Introduction ‣ Implicit Geometry of Next-token Prediction: From Language Sparsity Patterns to Model Representations"), where we compared the logits, word and context embeddings trained on Simplified TinyStories by (a) TF, (b) [NTP-UFM](#S3.Ex4 "In Definition 1 (NTP-UFM). ‣ 3.2 Unconstrained features model for NTP training ‣ 3 Formulation ‣ Implicit Geometry of Next-token Prediction: From Language Sparsity Patterns to Model Representations"), and those predicted by (c) Claims [(C1)](#S1.I1.ix1 "item (C1) ‣ 1.2 Summary of findings ‣ 1 Introduction ‣ Implicit Geometry of Next-token Prediction: From Language Sparsity Patterns to Model Representations")-[(C3)](#S1.I2.ix2 "item (C3) ‣ 1.2 Summary of findings ‣ 1 Introduction ‣ Implicit Geometry of Next-token Prediction: From Language Sparsity Patterns to Model Representations"), and (d) Proxy [(P)](#S1.I3.ix1 "item (P) ‣ 1.2 Summary of findings ‣ 1 Introduction ‣ Implicit Geometry of Next-token Prediction: From Language Sparsity Patterns to Model Representations"). Similar observations for the TinyStories and Synthetic datasets are shown in Figs. [2](#S1.F2 "Figure 2 ‣ 1.2 Summary of findings ‣ 1 Introduction ‣ Implicit Geometry of Next-token Prediction: From Language Sparsity Patterns to Model Representations") and [12](#A4.F12 "Figure 12 ‣ D.1.3 Additional results ‣ D.1 Additional details and results: Sec. 5 ‣ Appendix D Numerical experiments ‣ Implicit Geometry of Next-token Prediction: From Language Sparsity Patterns to Model Representations"). For visualization details, see App. [D.1](#A4.SS1 "D.1 Additional details and results: Sec. 5 ‣ Appendix D Numerical experiments ‣ Implicit Geometry of Next-token Prediction: From Language Sparsity Patterns to Model Representations").

Next, we discuss Fig. [6](#S5.F6 "Figure 6 ‣ 5.3 Metrics. ‣ 5 Experiments ‣ Implicit Geometry of Next-token Prediction: From Language Sparsity Patterns to Model Representations") that tracks our metrics during training for the Synthetic and Simplified TinyStories datasets. Fig. [6](#S5.F6 "Figure 6 ‣ 5.3 Metrics. ‣ 5 Experiments ‣ Implicit Geometry of Next-token Prediction: From Language Sparsity Patterns to Model Representations")-(a) confirms that the loss is close to the empirical entropy ℋℋ\mathcal{H}. Fig. [6](#S5.F6 "Figure 6 ‣ 5.3 Metrics. ‣ 5 Experiments ‣ Implicit Geometry of Next-token Prediction: From Language Sparsity Patterns to Model Representations")-(b) confirms the norm growth of the parameters. Note the slower growth of the context embeddings 𝑯𝑯{\bm{H}} in the TF model, which we suspect is due to the layer normalization at the final layer. We also confirm Claim [(C4)](#S1.I2.ix3 "item (C4) ‣ 1.2 Summary of findings ‣ 1 Introduction ‣ Implicit Geometry of Next-token Prediction: From Language Sparsity Patterns to Model Representations"), the recovery of the soft labels, in Fig. [6](#S5.F6 "Figure 6 ‣ 5.3 Metrics. ‣ 5 Experiments ‣ Implicit Geometry of Next-token Prediction: From Language Sparsity Patterns to Model Representations")-(c). At last, Fig. [6](#S5.F6 "Figure 6 ‣ 5.3 Metrics. ‣ 5 Experiments ‣ Implicit Geometry of Next-token Prediction: From Language Sparsity Patterns to Model Representations")-(d) and (e) track the correlation of the learned geometries with the proxy [(P)](#S1.I3.ix1 "item (P) ‣ 1.2 Summary of findings ‣ 1 Introduction ‣ Implicit Geometry of Next-token Prediction: From Language Sparsity Patterns to Model Representations") and with the theoretical predictions (𝑯mm,𝑾mm)superscript𝑯mmsuperscript𝑾mm({\bm{H}}^{\rm{mm}},{\bm{W}}^{\rm{mm}}). Note that all similarity metrics increase towards 111 as training progresses. In Figs. [13](#A4.F13 "Figure 13 ‣ D.1.3 Additional results ‣ D.1 Additional details and results: Sec. 5 ‣ Appendix D Numerical experiments ‣ Implicit Geometry of Next-token Prediction: From Language Sparsity Patterns to Model Representations") and [14](#A4.F14 "Figure 14 ‣ D.1.3 Additional results ‣ D.1 Additional details and results: Sec. 5 ‣ Appendix D Numerical experiments ‣ Implicit Geometry of Next-token Prediction: From Language Sparsity Patterns to Model Representations") in the appendix, we display the evolution of the embeddings by capturing their heatmaps at various training checkpoints. Interestingly, the final patterns emerge early in the training process. Fig. [6](#S5.F6 "Figure 6 ‣ 5.3 Metrics. ‣ 5 Experiments ‣ Implicit Geometry of Next-token Prediction: From Language Sparsity Patterns to Model Representations") displays analogous plots for TinyStories.

Finally, in Figs. [7](#S5.F7 "Figure 7 ‣ 5.4 Results ‣ 5 Experiments ‣ Implicit Geometry of Next-token Prediction: From Language Sparsity Patterns to Model Representations") and [15](#A4.F15 "Figure 15 ‣ D.1.3 Additional results ‣ D.1 Additional details and results: Sec. 5 ‣ Appendix D Numerical experiments ‣ Implicit Geometry of Next-token Prediction: From Language Sparsity Patterns to Model Representations"), we visualize the text values of the contexts and words along with their geometry heatmaps. Observe that the embeddings’ similarities are consistent with the linguistic properties of words/contexts: Embeddings of contexts such as ‘‘boy named Timmy. Timmy’’ and ‘‘kid called Lilly. She’’ align closely as they are likely to be followed by similar set of words (i.e., they have similar sparsity patterns). Also, word embeddings tend to cluster by their grammatical categories, with verbs and adjectives often showing high similarity. For instance, the verbs go, eat, sing, wear, play, and the adjectives clever, fat, furry, lively, little have high similarity. This contrasts with the maximally separated word embeddings in the unrealistic, perfectly symmetric setting analyzed in Section [4.4.1](#S4.SS4.SSS1 "4.4.1 Special case: support sets of equal sizes ‣ 4.4 On the solution of \"NTP-SVM\"_⋆ ‣ 4 Analysis of unconstrained features model for NTP training ‣ Implicit Geometry of Next-token Prediction: From Language Sparsity Patterns to Model Representations"), for which word embeddings where maximally separated. Here, the sparsity patterns of natural language induces an intricate geometry of word embeddings that accurately reflects linguistic structures such as grammatical categories.
