## 6 Concluding remarks: Limitations and future work

We have merged insights from the study of deep-net embeddings’ geometry in one-hot classification, implicit regularization theories, and a sparse soft-label classification framing of NTP, to arrive at a framework for examining word and context embeddings under the NTP paradigm. Key to our approach is the simplification to a problem of rank-constrained nuclear-norm-regularized CE minimization across logits (Eq. ([8](#S3.E8 "In Lemma 1. ‣ 3.3 Reformulation in terms of logits ‣ 3 Formulation ‣ Implicit Geometry of Next-token Prediction: From Language Sparsity Patterns to Model Representations"))). When the embedding dimension d𝑑d is large relative to the vocabulary size V𝑉V, we reveal how the similarities between word/context embeddings are heavily influenced by the sparsity patterns of the support sets. We do so by characterizing the implicit bias of NTP as it approaches the empirical entropy lower-bound leading to Claims [(C1)](#S1.I1.ix1 "item (C1) ‣ 1.2 Summary of findings ‣ 1 Introduction ‣ Implicit Geometry of Next-token Prediction: From Language Sparsity Patterns to Model Representations")-[(C4)](#S1.I2.ix3 "item (C4) ‣ 1.2 Summary of findings ‣ 1 Introduction ‣ Implicit Geometry of Next-token Prediction: From Language Sparsity Patterns to Model Representations").

Looking forward, we see several promising avenues for expanding upon our framework and addressing its present limitations:

First, we believe it is feasible to broaden the regularization-path analysis to the non-convex realm of problem ([8](#S3.E8 "In Lemma 1. ‣ 3.3 Reformulation in terms of logits ‣ 3 Formulation ‣ Implicit Geometry of Next-token Prediction: From Language Sparsity Patterns to Model Representations")), potentially revealing a rank-constrained variant of [NTP-SVM⋆subscriptNTP-SVM⋆\text{NTP-SVM}\_{\star}](#S4.Ex6 "In Definition 3. ‣ 4.1 NTP-SVM logits ‣ 4 Analysis of unconstrained features model for NTP training ‣ Implicit Geometry of Next-token Prediction: From Language Sparsity Patterns to Model Representations"). We hypothesize that the support matrices typical of natural language data might accommodate low-rank solutions that adhere to the constraints specified in Eqns. ([9](#S4.E9 "In Definition 3. ‣ 4.1 NTP-SVM logits ‣ 4 Analysis of unconstrained features model for NTP training ‣ Implicit Geometry of Next-token Prediction: From Language Sparsity Patterns to Model Representations"))-([10](#S4.E10 "In Definition 3. ‣ 4.1 NTP-SVM logits ‣ 4 Analysis of unconstrained features model for NTP training ‣ Implicit Geometry of Next-token Prediction: From Language Sparsity Patterns to Model Representations")). This would allow us to relax the d≥V𝑑𝑉d\geq V constraint in our analysis; see App. [D.4](#A4.SS4 "D.4 Discussion on large vocabulary setting ‣ Appendix D Numerical experiments ‣ Implicit Geometry of Next-token Prediction: From Language Sparsity Patterns to Model Representations") for more discussions. But even under the condition d≥V𝑑𝑉d\geq V several exciting directions remain open.
For example, investigating the implicit bias of GD—and potentially other optimizers like Adam, which are more commonly used in language model training but might present additional challenges—is important. In optimizing ([8](#S3.E8 "In Lemma 1. ‣ 3.3 Reformulation in terms of logits ‣ 3 Formulation ‣ Implicit Geometry of Next-token Prediction: From Language Sparsity Patterns to Model Representations")), does GD follow the regularization path? If not, to what extent does the solution learned by GD deviate from the sparse plus low-rank structure?

Second, while our initial focus was on establishing the framework, conducting preliminary analyses and proof-of-concept experiments, we encourage more extensive experimental exploration, especially with larger datasets and more complex model architectures with larger context window. Experiments in this paper focused on TF models due to their recent success in language. However, our theoretical results are independent of network design. Instead, they require the model to be over-parameterized enough to reach the entropy lower bound and ensure expressiveness in the embedding space. For an example, see App. [D.2](#A4.SS2 "D.2 Other architectures ‣ Appendix D Numerical experiments ‣ Implicit Geometry of Next-token Prediction: From Language Sparsity Patterns to Model Representations").

Third, it is intriguing to investigate how the distributional characteristics of word/context pairs in natural language, represented by the probability matrix 𝑷𝑷{\bm{P}}—and primarily by its support 𝑺𝑺\bm{S}—influence key properties like low-rankness and symmetry in the SVD factors of 𝑳mmsuperscript𝑳mm\bm{L}^{\rm{mm}}. Relatedly, we aim to leverage these insights to more effectively explore the empirical connection between 𝑳mmsuperscript𝑳mm\bm{L}^{\rm{mm}} and the centered support matrix 𝑺~~𝑺\widetilde{\bm{S}} complementing the heuristic arguments in Sec. [4.4.3](#S4.SS4.SSS3 "4.4.3 A proxy for \"NTP-SVM\"_⋆ solutions ‣ 4.4 On the solution of \"NTP-SVM\"_⋆ ‣ 4 Analysis of unconstrained features model for NTP training ‣ Implicit Geometry of Next-token Prediction: From Language Sparsity Patterns to Model Representations") in support of proxy [(P)](#S1.I3.ix1 "item (P) ‣ 1.2 Summary of findings ‣ 1 Introduction ‣ Implicit Geometry of Next-token Prediction: From Language Sparsity Patterns to Model Representations"). Considering this proxy, a low-rank structure of 𝑺~~𝑺\widetilde{\bm{S}} would also imply a low-rank nature for 𝑳mmsuperscript𝑳mm\bm{L}^{\rm{mm}} (currently only indirectly supported by low-rank minimization).
Fourth, although the framework encompasses full autoregressive training, our experimental study has focused on prediction of the last token prediction, with the exception of preliminary experiments in App. [D.3](#A4.SS3 "D.3 Auto-regressive training ‣ Appendix D Numerical experiments ‣ Implicit Geometry of Next-token Prediction: From Language Sparsity Patterns to Model Representations"). Deeper studies are required with sequences of variable length to empirically evaluate the brittleness of our model’s assumption, which enumerates distinct contexts regardless of their length or constituent token composition.

Fifth, our framework characterizes the geometry of word/context representations in a saturating regime where the NTP loss reaches its entropy lower-bound, suggesting that the model is sufficiently overparameterized. Although current state-of-the-art models do not reach this saturation regime and are likely not overparameterized, emerging evidence points to the need for extended training periods to achieve better generalization (Power et al., [2022](#bib.bib49)) or to learn complex behaviors like hierarchical structures (Murty et al., [2023](#bib.bib44)) and high-order in-context statistics (Edelman et al., [2024](#bib.bib12)). It’s also plausible that as models grow larger and training methods become more efficient, extended training periods may become feasible. Investigating the potential benefits of prolonged training could yield valuable insights, and we hypothesize that the implicit optimization biases of NTP, as discussed in Thrampoulidis ([2024](#bib.bib62)) and our work, offer a compelling framework for such exploration. Additionally, determining the degree of overparameterization required to achieve the entropy lower-bound presents an intriguing challenge in its own right Madden et al. ([2024](#bib.bib38)).

Ultimately, our goal is to spur further exploration into the geometries of context and word embeddings.
Akin to recent promising studies in image-classification that leverage the neural-collapse geometry, insights gained from such studies within NTP could inform the development of more effective loss functions, accelerate training processes, provide defenses against adversarial attacks, and guide the architectural choices for downstream tasks.
More broadly, we aim to foster interest in deeper investigations into how the inductive bias of NTP influences the learning of linguistic patterns and regularities.

## Appendix A Related work: Detailed version

This section expands upon the section on related work in the main body.

### A.1 Word embeddings as matrix factorization

Neural probabilistic language models are designed to predict the probability distribution of a target word v∈𝒱𝑣𝒱v\in\mathcal{V} within a given vocabulary
𝒱𝒱\mathcal{V}, based on a sequence of preceding or surrounding words of (variable or fixed) length t𝑡t known as the context 𝒛∈𝒱t𝒛superscript𝒱𝑡\bm{z}\in\mathcal{V}^{t}. In these models, word and context representations (aka embeddings) are parameterized by their internal architecture (Bengio et al., [2000](#bib.bib7)). The emergence of energy-based models like the Word2Vec family, particularly through the introduction of training methodologies such as the Skip-Gram with Negative Sampling (SGNS) objective by Mikolov et al. ([2013b](#bib.bib40); [a](#bib.bib39)), marked a significant advancement in learning high quality embeddings; e.g., see Jurafsky & Martin ([2023](#bib.bib32)). SGNS aims to optimize the alignment between vectors of *target* words and the *context* words surrounding the target, while disassociating those of randomly drawn pairs. This is achieved by optimizing a log-bilinear model that learns unconstrained embeddings for words in two roles: as targets and as contexts. While intuitive, the objective remained somewhat mysterious until the seminal analysis by Levy & Goldberg ([2014](#bib.bib33)) offered a formal examination of embeddings generated by the SGNS objective, framing it as a form of weighted matrix factorization. Specifically for embeddings of large dimensionality, Levy and Goldberg demonstrated that SGNS implicitly factorizes a shifted version of the Pointwise Mutual Information (PMI) matrix, denoted as
PMI, which is a measure of association between words and contexts by having entries PMI​[z,j]=log⁡Pr​(z|j)Pr​(z)=log⁡p^j,zPr​(z)PMI𝑧𝑗PrconditionalzjPrzsubscript^𝑝

𝑗𝑧Prz\textbf{PMI}[z,j]=\log\frac{\rm{Pr}(z|j)}{\rm{Pr}(z)}=\log\frac{\hat{p}\_{j,z}}{\rm{Pr}(z)} for target word z𝑧z and context word j𝑗j. This result establishes a fundamental connection between count-based and predictive models (Pennington et al., [2014](#bib.bib47)). However, this approach presumes non-zero occurrences of all context-word pairs in the training set, a condition not always met, leading to undefined factorization for pairs with zero co-occurrence (resulting in
PMI​[z,j]=−∞PMI𝑧𝑗\textbf{PMI}[z,j]=-\infty). To address the possibility of negative infinity values, an approximation involving a positive, sparse, and non-negative PMI (PPMI) matrix was proposed. While this adaptation offered an intriguing alternative to the original training objectives, the geometric characteristics of the resultant embeddings in comparison to those derived from the original loss remain unclear.

Our approach mirrors that of Levy & Goldberg ([2014](#bib.bib33)) to some extent (see also Pennington et al. ([2014](#bib.bib47))), especially in treating NTP as a soft-label classification across distinct contexts and presuming unconstrained context embeddings (although these concepts are not clearly mentioned in those original works). At a very basic level our work distinguishes itself by focusing on the NTP objective, which, although akin to SGNS and NC objectives, employs cross-entropy loss instead of sigmoid functions. Aside from this distinction, we identify the following conceptual divergences, contributing to our novel perspective on this line of inquiry: (i) Firstly, we aim to characterize the geometry of embeddings as learned directly from the loss function, rather than through approximations.
(ii) Secondly, although [NTP-UFM](#S3.Ex4 "In Definition 1 (NTP-UFM). ‣ 3.2 Unconstrained features model for NTP training ‣ 3 Formulation ‣ Implicit Geometry of Next-token Prediction: From Language Sparsity Patterns to Model Representations"), as a log-bilinear model, is in spirit to the log-bilinear models trained by Word2Vec, we only use such log-bilinear models as an analytically-tractable proxy to expressive deep neural probabilistic language models such as transformers.
(iii) Thirdly, we confront the sparsity in the probabilistic labels of each context head-on, unlike other matrix-factorization methods that circumvent sparsity in a heuristic manner through smoothing or weighting techniques (Levy & Goldberg, [2014](#bib.bib33); Pennington et al., [2014](#bib.bib47)). This helps us identify that sparsity leads to diverging embeddings, a scenario where setting the loss gradient to zero is infeasible with finite weights.
(iv) Fourthly, by examining embeddings through the lens of the regularization path—a surrogate for gradient descent optimization—we unveil that word and context embeddings emerge from the factorization of a matrix
𝑳in+R​𝑳mmsuperscript𝑳in𝑅superscript𝑳mm\bm{L}^{\rm{in}}+R\bm{L}^{\rm{mm}}. Here,
𝑳insuperscript𝑳in\bm{L}^{\rm{in}} captures the frequency of word-context occurrences akin to the PMI, setting non-occurring pairs to zero while retaining positive values for occurring pairs, much like the PPMI matrix. The directional component
𝑳mmsuperscript𝑳mm\bm{L}^{\rm{mm}}, becoming dominant as weights diverge, reflects the explicit sparsity patterns of the context-word probability matrix.

The above-mentioned conceptual novelties can also be directly applied to the SGNS objective generalizing the result of Levy & Goldberg ([2014](#bib.bib33)) to sparse language patterns. We show this in App. [A.4](#A1.SS4 "A.4 Connections to Word2Vec ‣ Appendix A Related work: Detailed version ‣ Implicit Geometry of Next-token Prediction: From Language Sparsity Patterns to Model Representations").

The seminal contribution of Levy & Goldberg ([2014](#bib.bib33)) has inspired numerous subsequent studies, leveraging the geometric insights of embeddings to uncover linguistic phenomena such as the linear relationships underlying word analogies (Allen & Hospedales, [2019](#bib.bib1); Hashimoto et al., [2016](#bib.bib25); Arora et al., [2016](#bib.bib2); Ethayarajh et al., [2018](#bib.bib15); Mikolov et al., [2013c](#bib.bib41)).
Other works, investigate the *anisotropy* of the learned word/context embeddings for both static models such as Word2Vec (Mu et al., [2017](#bib.bib43)) and contextualized models such as pre-trained BERT and GPT-2 (Ethayarajh, [2019](#bib.bib14); Li et al., [2020](#bib.bib34)). This property of the embeddings has been attributed to the imbalanced long-tail distribution of language (Gao et al., [2019](#bib.bib19); Li et al., [2020](#bib.bib34)), shifts in the embedding space (Biś et al., [2021](#bib.bib8)), and characteristic of the network architecture (Godey et al., [2024](#bib.bib20)). Various techniques have been proposed to improve the isotropy of the embeddings, which yields embeddings with better language regularities (e.g., (Mu et al., [2017](#bib.bib43); Gao et al., [2019](#bib.bib19); Biś et al., [2021](#bib.bib8); Timkey & Van Schijndel, [2021](#bib.bib64))).
We envision that our fresh perspective on geometric analysis and our findings could similarly motivate further research in this domain, extending the understanding and application of language model embeddings.

Finally, we note that a *sparse plus low-rank* structure for parameters learned with weighted exponential language models trained on fixed n-gram word/context features has been observed in Hutchinson et al. ([2012](#bib.bib26)). Through experiments, the authors decompose the weights learned by optimizing a weighted maximum entropy objective into sparse and low-rank components. They demonstrate that explicitly incorporating sparsity and low-rank constraints into their weighted maximum entropy framework can enhance both performance and interpretability (Hutchinson et al., [2012](#bib.bib26); [2013](#bib.bib27)), particularly when training data is limited (Hutchinson et al., [2015](#bib.bib28)). However, these components serve different roles compared to our work. In their framework, the low-rank component captures frequent word co-occurrences, while the sparse component records infrequent words and special lexical items. In contrast, our analysis shows via the regularization path framework that the NTP objective produces a low-rank component from a nuclear-norm max-margin problem between in-support and out-support tokens, with the sparse component encoding the frequency of the in-support tokens.

### A.2 Overparameterization and Implicit bias/regularization of GD

Recent studies on gradient-based optimization methods, such as SGD and its variants, have focused on overparameterized settings in empirical risk minimization without explicit regularization. This interest stems from observations that deep neural networks, especially in image classification, perform exceptionally well even with minimal or no weight decay. This research area, known as “implicit bias” or “implicit regularization” of GD emerged to explore which among the numerous potential minimizers in overparameterized settings are preferred by algorithms like GD. Notably, Soudry et al. ([2018](#bib.bib57)); Ji & Telgarsky ([2018](#bib.bib29)) showed that for linear logistic regression with one-hot labels, GD aligns with the hard-margin SVM solution, essentially becoming the max-margin classifier. Additionally, Ji & Telgarsky ([2020](#bib.bib30)) indicated that GD’s optimization path in linear models is analogous to the regularization path, which looks at risk minimizers as regularization diminishes. This path is analytically more accessible and offers a useful framework to study GD’s behavior. In our work, we apply this idea to investigate the regularization path of NTP training.
These foundational results on implicit bias have spurred further research into stochastic and adaptive gradient methods (Nacson et al., [2019](#bib.bib45); Pesme et al., [2021](#bib.bib48); Sun et al., [2022](#bib.bib59); Azizan et al., [2021](#bib.bib3); Cattaneo et al., [2023](#bib.bib10)), and more intricate architectures (Lyu & Li, [2020](#bib.bib37); Ji & Telgarsky, [2020](#bib.bib30); Gunasekar et al., [2018a](#bib.bib22); [b](#bib.bib23))) including recent studies on transformers (Tarzanagh et al., [2023b](#bib.bib61); [a](#bib.bib60)); for a review, refer to Vardi & Shamir ([2021](#bib.bib66)). These insights have also paved the way for generalization analysis, linking GD’s generalization to that of SVM-like solutions. This connection has been explored in high-dimensional contexts where traditional margin-based bounds are inadequate, revealing scenarios where no regularization yields optimal outcomes, a phenomenon known as ’harmless interpolation’ or ’benign overfitting.’ Most of these findings, whether on optimization or generalization, are confined to the one-hot classification framework.

In the language modeling literature, several theoretical works suggest that achieving the empirical entropy lower-bound at the pre-training stage results in better down-stream performance (Liu et al., [2023](#bib.bib36); Saunshi et al., [2020](#bib.bib54)). Liu et al. ([2023](#bib.bib36)) attributes this better down-stream performance to the implicit bias of the pre-training algorithms, by showing that the optimizers prefer the more transferable models among the ones that achieve the same minimal loss value.
A formal deviation from one-hot to soft-label classification for NTP is the work by (Thrampoulidis, [2024](#bib.bib62)), which partially inspired our research. While their analysis, assuming fixed context embeddings, applies to linear models, our work extends this by optimizing both word and context embeddings, leading to a nonlinear model. We discover that this nonlinearity yields a preference towards minimizing the nuclear norm of the logits over the Euclidean norm of the word embedding matrix, as seen in (Thrampoulidis, [2024](#bib.bib62)). Our theoretical contributions thus expand on their findings into the nonconvex domain.

### A.3 Neural collapse geometry

A third area of research closely related to our work is the investigation of neural-collapse (NC) geometry, which seeks to understand the geometry of last-layer features and classifier weights in deep networks optimized in the interpolating regime. Initiated by Papyan et al. ([2020](#bib.bib46)), this line of research uncovered a universal phenomenon across various architectures and datasets, where classifiers and last-layer features consistently converge to an equiangular tight frame (ETF). This convergence subsumes the aggregation of same-class features around their class mean, a phenomenon termed NC. The theoretical underpinnings for these empirical findings were later provided by analyzing the unconstrained features model (UFM) (Mixon et al., [2020](#bib.bib42); Fang et al., [2021](#bib.bib16); Zhu et al., [2021](#bib.bib70)), suggesting that highly overparameterized networks can produce arbitrary last-layer features. This concept, reminiscent of the early framework used in Word2Vec models (Levy & Goldberg, [2014](#bib.bib33)) as discussed above (see also Yang et al. ([2017](#bib.bib69))), has since spurred extensive research in diverse contexts.

Among the vast body of work on NC geometry–an exhaustive reference of which is beyond our scope–, two studies stand out for their relevance to our research. Thrampoulidis et al. ([2022](#bib.bib63)) first adapted the UFM to describe geometries diverging from symmetric ETF by moving away from the balanced data assumption, demonstrating that logits optimizing the UFM with vanishing regularization align with a nuclear-norm SVM problem; see also Behnia et al. ([2023](#bib.bib5)). This approach also bridges NC geometry with the implicit bias literature previously discussed. Our work extends their principles to the NTP objective, differing from the one-hot encoding classification focus of their study. We take a similar approach, but this time applied to the NTP objective. This is unlike their result which only applies to one-hot encoding classification. In fact, our formulation in [NTP-SVM⋆subscriptNTP-SVM⋆\text{NTP-SVM}\_{\star}](#S4.Ex6 "In Definition 3. ‣ 4.1 NTP-SVM logits ‣ 4 Analysis of unconstrained features model for NTP training ‣ Implicit Geometry of Next-token Prediction: From Language Sparsity Patterns to Model Representations") extends their findings, encompassing them as a specific instance when each context is followed by only one token—a scenario less applicable to the linguistic contexts we focus on. Moreover, we demonstrate (see Theorem [2](#Thmtheorem2 "Theorem 2. ‣ 4.2 Regularization-path analysis of logits ‣ 4 Analysis of unconstrained features model for NTP training ‣ Implicit Geometry of Next-token Prediction: From Language Sparsity Patterns to Model Representations")) that the NTP framework introduces a finite projection component to the regularization path, a feature absent in one-hot settings.

Our findings also resonate to certain extent with the recent multilabel NC geometry study in Li et al. ([2023](#bib.bib35)), where each example, typically an image, is tagged with multiple correct labels, represented as a k𝑘k-hot vector. In contrast, our NTP framework, modeled as soft-label classification, associates each context with a probability vector. This distinction makes our framework more versatile, applicable beyond the restrictive case where soft-label classification aligns with multi-label classification—specifically, when all relevant tokens are equally likely. There are also crucial differences in the outcomes. Their analysis assumes an equal number of training samples of multiplicity k=1𝑘1k=1 for each category, a condition that, in our context, would necessitate every vocabulary word to be followed by an identical number of contexts that are only followed by that word—a significantly limiting constraint. Our findings, however, are not bound by this assumption.

In an attempt to extend this line of work to language setups, contemporaneous work Wu & Papyan ([2024](#bib.bib68)), through experiments, suggests a correlation between the degree of convergence to the NC geometry and the validation loss for different language models trained on TinyStories for a few epochs. Different to us, they implicitly treat the NTP objective as one-hot classification and suggest that better *per-class* (i.e., next-token) collapse of the context embeddings leads to improved validation loss. In contrast, our analysis reveals a more intricate finding: within a sparse soft-label formulation of NTP training with language, it is not *per-class* collapse that minimizes the training objective. Instead, the objective is minimized when context embeddings with the same *support set* collapse after being projected onto *subspace* ℱ⟂subscriptℱperpendicular-to\mathscr{F}\_{\perp}, which is defined by the sparsity pattern of the context-word co-occurrence matrix (see Sec. [4.3](#S4.SS3 "4.3 Geometry of embeddings ‣ 4 Analysis of unconstrained features model for NTP training ‣ Implicit Geometry of Next-token Prediction: From Language Sparsity Patterns to Model Representations")). Our approach also comes with an analysis framework rather than relying solely on experiments.

The logit matrix 𝑳1subscript𝑳1\bm{L}\_{1} is the shifted PMI matrix as also characterized in Levy & Goldberg ([2014](#bib.bib33), Eq. (7)). This component plays a similar role to the finite component 𝑳insuperscript𝑳in\bm{L}^{\rm{in}} in the NTP setup of our paper. The second component, which is only non-zero if there exists a target-context pair (z,j)𝑧𝑗(z,j) with zero co-occurrence in the training set (Pr(j,z)=0)\Pr(j,z)=0), specifies the directional component, akin to the role of 𝑳mmsuperscript𝑳mm\bm{L}^{\rm{mm}} in NTP. This component is absent in the analysis of Levy & Goldberg ([2014](#bib.bib33)), as they implicitly assume no zero entries in the co-occurence matrix when setting the derivative of the loss to zero. However, when the co-occurrence matrix is sparse, zero derivation of the loss is not achieved at any finite solution. Instead, 𝑳2subscript𝑳2\bm{L}\_{2} identifies the infinite direction that drives the loss to its lower-bound. Levy & Goldberg ([2014](#bib.bib33)) address sparsity indirectly by discussing alternative matrix factorizations such as shifted positive-PMI (PPMI).

   The word embedding matrix 𝑾𝑾{\bm{W}} is centered motivating the use of the *centered* support matrix 𝑺~~𝑺\widetilde{\bm{S}} (rather than simply 𝑺𝑺\bm{S}) in Proxy [(P)](#S1.I3.ix1 "item (P) ‣ 1.2 Summary of findings ‣ 1 Introduction ‣ Implicit Geometry of Next-token Prediction: From Language Sparsity Patterns to Model Representations").

### B.2 Proof of Lemma [1](#Thmlemma1 "Lemma 1. ‣ 3.3 Reformulation in terms of logits ‣ 3 Formulation ‣ Implicit Geometry of Next-token Prediction: From Language Sparsity Patterns to Model Representations")

We prove a slightly stronger version of Lemma [1](#Thmlemma1 "Lemma 1. ‣ 3.3 Reformulation in terms of logits ‣ 3 Formulation ‣ Implicit Geometry of Next-token Prediction: From Language Sparsity Patterns to Model Representations") which assumes the additional constraint 𝟙⊤​𝑳=0superscript1top𝑳0\mathds{1}^{\top}\bm{L}=0 in Eq. [NTP-UFM](#S3.Ex4 "In Definition 1 (NTP-UFM). ‣ 3.2 Unconstrained features model for NTP training ‣ 3 Formulation ‣ Implicit Geometry of Next-token Prediction: From Language Sparsity Patterns to Model Representations"); see Lemma [2](#Thmlemma2 "Lemma 2. ‣ B.1 Centering of word embeddings ‣ Appendix B Proofs ‣ Implicit Geometry of Next-token Prediction: From Language Sparsity Patterns to Model Representations"). The proof follows identically if we ignore this centering property.

### B.3 Proof of Theorems [1](#Thmtheorem1 "Theorem 1. ‣ 4.2 Regularization-path analysis of logits ‣ 4 Analysis of unconstrained features model for NTP training ‣ Implicit Geometry of Next-token Prediction: From Language Sparsity Patterns to Model Representations") and [2](#Thmtheorem2 "Theorem 2. ‣ 4.2 Regularization-path analysis of logits ‣ 4 Analysis of unconstrained features model for NTP training ‣ Implicit Geometry of Next-token Prediction: From Language Sparsity Patterns to Model Representations")

#### B.3.2 When does the NTP loss attain its entropy lower-bound?

For word/context embeddings 𝑾,𝑯

𝑾𝑯{\bm{W}},{\bm{H}} and logits 𝑳=𝑾​𝑯𝑳𝑾𝑯\bm{L}={\bm{W}}{\bm{H}}, recall the NTP loss is written as

|  |  |  |  |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
|  | CE⁡(𝑾​𝑯)=CE⁡(𝑳)=∑j∈[m]π^j​∑z∈𝒮jp^j,z​log⁡(1+∑z′≠z∈𝒮je−(𝑳​[z,j]−𝑳​[z′,j])+∑v∉𝒮je−(𝑳​[z,j]−𝑳​[v,j])),CE𝑾𝑯CE𝑳subscript𝑗delimited-[]𝑚subscript^𝜋𝑗subscript𝑧subscript𝒮𝑗subscript^𝑝  𝑗𝑧1subscriptsuperscript𝑧′𝑧subscript𝒮𝑗superscript𝑒𝑳𝑧𝑗𝑳superscript𝑧′𝑗subscript𝑣subscript𝒮𝑗superscript𝑒𝑳𝑧𝑗𝑳𝑣𝑗\displaystyle\operatorname{CE}({\bm{W}}{\bm{H}})=\operatorname{CE}(\bm{L})=\sum\_{j\in[m]}\hat{\pi}\_{j}\sum\_{z\in{\mathcal{S}}\_{j}}\hat{p}\_{j,z}\log\left(1+\sum\_{z^{\prime}\neq z\in{\mathcal{S}}\_{j}}e^{-(\bm{L}[z,j]-\bm{L}[z^{\prime},j])}+\sum\_{v\not\in{\mathcal{S}}\_{j}}e^{-(\bm{L}[z,j]-\bm{L}[v,j])}\right)\,, |  | (21) |

and is lower bounded by the empirical T𝑇T-gram entropy of the data (Shannon, [1948](#bib.bib56)), i.e., for all 𝜽′superscript𝜽′\bm{\theta}^{\prime}:
CE⁡(𝑾​𝑯)≥ℋ:=𝔼^(𝒙,z)∼𝒯n​[−log⁡(p^​(z|𝒙))].CE𝑾𝑯ℋassignsubscript^𝔼similar-to𝒙𝑧subscript𝒯𝑛delimited-[]^𝑝conditional𝑧𝒙\operatorname{CE}({\bm{W}}{\bm{H}})\geq\mathcal{H}:=\hat{\mathbb{E}}\_{(\bm{x},z)\sim\mathcal{T}\_{n}}\left[-\log\left(\hat{p}(z|\bm{x})\right)\right]\,.

We now state explicit conditions on the logit matrix 𝑳𝑳\bm{L} for which the lower bound is attained by a softmax model 𝑳=𝕊​(𝑾​𝑯)𝑳𝕊𝑾𝑯\bm{L}=\mathbb{S}({\bm{W}}{\bm{H}}) with unconstrained context embeddings 𝑯𝑯{\bm{H}}. Due to sparsity of the conditional probability matrix 𝑷𝑷{\bm{P}}, the lower bound is *not* attained for any
finite logit matrix, but it can be reached asymptotically provided the conditions stated in the proposition below hold.

The statement below is a slight extension of Thrampoulidis ([2024](#bib.bib62), Prop. 1).

###### Proposition 4.

The NTP objective of a softmax model 𝕊​(𝐖​𝐇)𝕊𝐖𝐇\mathbb{S}({\bm{W}}{\bm{H}}) with 𝐖∈ℝV×d,𝐇∈ℝd×mformulae-sequence𝐖superscriptℝ𝑉𝑑𝐇superscriptℝ𝑑𝑚{\bm{W}}\in\mathbb{R}^{V\times d},{\bm{H}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times m} can reach the empirical entropy lower-bound if the following three conditions hold simultaneously

1. A.

   [NTPH-compatibility] There exists logit matrix 𝑳1∈ℝV×msubscript𝑳1superscriptℝ𝑉𝑚\bm{L}\_{1}\in\mathbb{R}^{V\times m} such that

   |  |  |  |  |
   | --- | --- | --- | --- |
   |  | ∀j∈[m],z≠z′∈𝒮j:𝑳1​[j,z]−𝑳1​[j,z′]=log⁡(p^j,zp^j,z′).:formulae-sequencefor-all𝑗delimited-[]𝑚𝑧superscript𝑧′subscript𝒮𝑗subscript𝑳1𝑗𝑧subscript𝑳1𝑗superscript𝑧′subscript^𝑝 𝑗𝑧subscript^𝑝 𝑗superscript𝑧′\displaystyle\forall j\in[m],z\neq z^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{S}}\_{j}\,:\,\bm{L}\_{1}[j,z]-\bm{L}\_{1}[j,z^{\prime}]=\log\left(\frac{\hat{p}\_{j,z}}{\hat{p}\_{j,z^{\prime}}}\right)\,. |  | (22) |
2. B.

   [NTP-separability] There exists logit matrix 𝑳2∈ℝV×msubscript𝑳2superscriptℝ𝑉𝑚\bm{L}\_{2}\in\mathbb{R}^{V\times m} that is feasible in [NTP-SVM⋆subscriptNTP-SVMitalic-⋆\text{NTP-SVM}\_{\star}](#S4.Ex6 "In Definition 3. ‣ 4.1 NTP-SVM logits ‣ 4 Analysis of unconstrained features model for NTP training ‣ Implicit Geometry of Next-token Prediction: From Language Sparsity Patterns to Model Representations").
3. C.

   [Rank constraint] For all arbitrarily large positive scalars ρ𝜌\rho,

The challenge is in satisfying conditions *A*-*C* simultaneously.

However, when d≥V𝑑𝑉d\geq V, then the rank condition *C* of the theorem is trivial. Thus, we have the following corollary.

###### Corollary 2.

Suppose d≥V𝑑𝑉d\geq V, then the entropy lower bound can be asymptotically attained by the NTP loss.

#### B.3.6 Proof of Theorem [3](#Thmtheorem3 "Theorem 3. ‣ B.3.3 Ball-constrained CE minimization and main result ‣ B.3 Proof of Theorems 1 and 2 ‣ Appendix B Proofs ‣ Implicit Geometry of Next-token Prediction: From Language Sparsity Patterns to Model Representations")

We prove each statement separately.

Statement (i). Since for all 𝑳𝑳\bm{L}, it holds CE⁡(𝑳)≥ℋCE𝑳ℋ\operatorname{CE}(\bm{L})\geq\mathcal{H}, it suffices to prove that ℋℋ\mathcal{H} can be asymptotically attained. Since d≥V𝑑𝑉d\geq V, this follows from Corollary [2](#Thmcorollary2 "Corollary 2. ‣ B.3.2 When does the NTP loss attain its entropy lower-bound? ‣ B.3 Proof of Theorems 1 and 2 ‣ Appendix B Proofs ‣ Implicit Geometry of Next-token Prediction: From Language Sparsity Patterns to Model Representations"). (For a more explicit proof, see Lemma [7](#Thmlemma7 "Lemma 7. ‣ B.3.5 Auxiliary lemmas ‣ B.3 Proof of Theorems 1 and 2 ‣ Appendix B Proofs ‣ Implicit Geometry of Next-token Prediction: From Language Sparsity Patterns to Model Representations")).

Statement (ii).  Since for all 𝑳𝑳\bm{L} it holds CE⁡(𝑳)≥CEin⁡(𝑳)≥CEin,⋆=ℋCE𝑳subscriptCEin𝑳subscriptCE

in⋆ℋ\operatorname{CE}(\bm{L})\geq\operatorname{CE}\_{\rm{in}}(\bm{L})\geq\operatorname{CE}\_{\rm{in},\star}=\mathcal{H}, it follows from Statement (i) that

|  |  |  |
| --- | --- | --- |
|  | limB→∞CEin⁡(𝑳^B)=CEin,⋆.subscript→𝐵subscriptCEinsubscript^𝑳𝐵subscriptCE  in⋆\lim\_{{B\rightarrow\infty}}\operatorname{CE}\_{\rm{in}}(\widehat{\bm{L}}\_{B})=\operatorname{CE}\_{\rm{in},\star}\,. |  |

But
CEin⁡(𝑳)=CEin⁡(𝒫ℱ​(𝑳))subscriptCEin𝑳subscriptCEinsubscript𝒫ℱ𝑳\operatorname{CE}\_{\rm{in}}(\bm{L})=\operatorname{CE}\_{\rm{in}}(\mathcal{P}\_{\mathscr{F}}(\bm{L})) (see Eq. ([27](#A2.E27 "In B.3.4 The in-support NTP loss and its minimizer 𝑳ⁱⁿ ‣ B.3 Proof of Theorems 1 and 2 ‣ Appendix B Proofs ‣ Implicit Geometry of Next-token Prediction: From Language Sparsity Patterns to Model Representations"))). Thus,

|  |  |  |
| --- | --- | --- |

The desired now follows ince 𝑳insuperscript𝑳in\bm{L}^{\rm{in}} is unique minimizer of CEinsubscriptCEin\operatorname{CE}\_{\rm{in}} on 𝒯𝒯\mathcal{T} by Lemma [4](#Thmlemma4 "Lemma 4. ‣ B.3.4 The in-support NTP loss and its minimizer 𝑳ⁱⁿ ‣ B.3 Proof of Theorems 1 and 2 ‣ Appendix B Proofs ‣ Implicit Geometry of Next-token Prediction: From Language Sparsity Patterns to Model Representations").

Statement (iii). Follows directly from Lemma [6](#Thmlemma6 "Lemma 6. ‣ B.3.5 Auxiliary lemmas ‣ B.3 Proof of Theorems 1 and 2 ‣ Appendix B Proofs ‣ Implicit Geometry of Next-token Prediction: From Language Sparsity Patterns to Model Representations").

Statement (iv). 
From Lemma [7](#Thmlemma7 "Lemma 7. ‣ B.3.5 Auxiliary lemmas ‣ B.3 Proof of Theorems 1 and 2 ‣ Appendix B Proofs ‣ Implicit Geometry of Next-token Prediction: From Language Sparsity Patterns to Model Representations"), there exists 𝑳Rsubscript𝑳𝑅\bm{L}\_{R} for which the loss is close to ℋℋ\mathcal{H} (see Eq. ([33](#A2.E33 "In Lemma 7. ‣ B.3.5 Auxiliary lemmas ‣ B.3 Proof of Theorems 1 and 2 ‣ Appendix B Proofs ‣ Implicit Geometry of Next-token Prediction: From Language Sparsity Patterns to Model Representations"))).

Towards arriving at a contradiction,
we will show that if 𝑳^Bsubscript^𝑳𝐵\widehat{\bm{L}}\_{B} is not in the direction of 𝑳mmsuperscript𝑳mm\bm{L}^{\rm{mm}}, then it incurs a loss that is larger than the upper bound of CE⁡(𝑳R)CEsubscript𝑳𝑅\operatorname{CE}(\bm{L}\_{R}) computed in Eq. ([33](#A2.E33 "In Lemma 7. ‣ B.3.5 Auxiliary lemmas ‣ B.3 Proof of Theorems 1 and 2 ‣ Appendix B Proofs ‣ Implicit Geometry of Next-token Prediction: From Language Sparsity Patterns to Model Representations")).

By assumption 𝑳mmsuperscript𝑳mm\bm{L}^{\rm{mm}} is optimal in [NTP-SVM⋆subscriptNTP-SVM⋆\text{NTP-SVM}\_{\star}](#S4.Ex6 "In Definition 3. ‣ 4.1 NTP-SVM logits ‣ 4 Analysis of unconstrained features model for NTP training ‣ Implicit Geometry of Next-token Prediction: From Language Sparsity Patterns to Model Representations"). We now show that the other two matrices are also optimal.

Firstly, note that 𝑳flipmmsubscriptsuperscript𝑳mmflip\bm{L}^{\rm{mm}}\_{\rm{flip}} is feasible in [NTP-SVM⋆subscriptNTP-SVM⋆\text{NTP-SVM}\_{\star}](#S4.Ex6 "In Definition 3. ‣ 4.1 NTP-SVM logits ‣ 4 Analysis of unconstrained features model for NTP training ‣ Implicit Geometry of Next-token Prediction: From Language Sparsity Patterns to Model Representations") because: (i) the affine constraints only depend on the support sets, which are same for j,j′​s

𝑗superscript𝑗′𝑠j,j^{\prime}s, (ii) rank⁡(𝑳flipmm)=rank⁡(𝑳mm)≤dranksubscriptsuperscript𝑳mmflipranksuperscript𝑳mm𝑑\operatorname{rank}\left(\bm{L}^{\rm{mm}}\_{\rm{flip}}\right)=\operatorname{rank}\left(\bm{L}^{\rm{mm}}\right)\leq d since permuting columns is rank-preserving operation. Moreover, permuting columns also preserves nuclear-norm of a matrix. To see this note that 𝑳flipmm=𝑳mm​𝑷=𝑼​𝚺​(𝑷⊤​𝑽)⊤subscriptsuperscript𝑳mmflipsuperscript𝑳mm𝑷𝑼𝚺superscriptsuperscript𝑷top𝑽top\bm{L}^{\rm{mm}}\_{\rm{flip}}=\bm{L}^{\rm{mm}}{\bm{P}}=\bm{U}\bm{\Sigma}({\bm{P}}^{\top}{\bm{V}})^{\top} for some permutation matrix 𝑷∈ℝm×m𝑷superscriptℝ𝑚𝑚{\bm{P}}\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times m}. Since 𝑽𝑽{\bm{V}} is partial orthonormal, the same is true for 𝑷⊤​𝑽superscript𝑷top𝑽{\bm{P}}^{\top}{\bm{V}}. Thus, ‖𝑳flipmm‖∗=tr⁡(𝚺)=‖𝑳mm‖∗subscriptnormsubscriptsuperscript𝑳mmfliptr𝚺subscriptnormsuperscript𝑳mm\|{\bm{L}^{\rm{mm}}\_{\rm{flip}}}\|\_{\*}=\operatorname{tr}({\bm{\Sigma}})=\|{\bm{L}^{\rm{mm}}}\|\_{\*}, which proves optimality of rank⁡(𝑳flipmm)ranksubscriptsuperscript𝑳mmflip\operatorname{rank}\left(\bm{L}^{\rm{mm}}\_{\rm{flip}}\right).

Similarly, we can argue that 𝑳avgmmsubscriptsuperscript𝑳mmavg\bm{L}^{\rm{mm}}\_{\rm{avg}} is feasible in [NTP-SVM⋆subscriptNTP-SVM⋆\text{NTP-SVM}\_{\star}](#S4.Ex6 "In Definition 3. ‣ 4.1 NTP-SVM logits ‣ 4 Analysis of unconstrained features model for NTP training ‣ Implicit Geometry of Next-token Prediction: From Language Sparsity Patterns to Model Representations") because: (i) the affine constraints only depend on the support sets, which are same for j,j′​s

𝑗superscript𝑗′𝑠j,j^{\prime}s, (ii) rank⁡(𝑳avgmm)=rank⁡(𝑳mm)≤dranksubscriptsuperscript𝑳mmavgranksuperscript𝑳mm𝑑\operatorname{rank}\left(\bm{L}^{\rm{mm}}\_{\rm{avg}}\right)=\operatorname{rank}\left(\bm{L}^{\rm{mm}}\right)\leq d since adding columns and multiplying them by scalars are rank-preserving operations. But now, from convexity of the objective function, and noting that 𝑳avgmm=12​(𝑳mm+𝑳flipmm)subscriptsuperscript𝑳mmavg12superscript𝑳mmsubscriptsuperscript𝑳mmflip\bm{L}^{\rm{mm}}\_{\rm{avg}}=\frac{1}{2}\left(\bm{L}^{\rm{mm}}+\bm{L}^{\rm{mm}}\_{\rm{flip}}\right) we have

|  |  |  |
| --- | --- | --- |
|  | ‖𝑳avgmm‖∗≤12​‖𝑳mm‖∗+12​‖𝑳flipmm‖∗=‖𝑳mm‖∗.subscriptnormsubscriptsuperscript𝑳mmavg12subscriptnormsuperscript𝑳mm12subscriptnormsubscriptsuperscript𝑳mmflipsubscriptnormsuperscript𝑳mm\|\bm{L}^{\rm{mm}}\_{\rm{avg}}\|\_{\*}\leq\frac{1}{2}\|\bm{L}^{\rm{mm}}\|\_{\*}+\frac{1}{2}\|\bm{L}^{\rm{mm}}\_{\rm{flip}}\|\_{\*}=\|\bm{L}^{\rm{mm}}\|\_{\*}\,. |  |

Thus, 𝑳avgmmsubscriptsuperscript𝑳mmavg\bm{L}^{\rm{mm}}\_{\rm{avg}} is also optimal.

Now, from Lemma [1](#Thmlemma1 "Lemma 1. ‣ 3.3 Reformulation in terms of logits ‣ 3 Formulation ‣ Implicit Geometry of Next-token Prediction: From Language Sparsity Patterns to Model Representations"), we know that for some partial orthonormal matrix 𝐑𝐑\mathbf{R}, in the limit of vanishing regularization: 𝑯λ∝𝐑⊤​𝚺12​𝑽⊤proportional-tosubscript𝑯𝜆superscript𝐑topsuperscript𝚺12superscript𝑽top{{\bm{H}}\_{\lambda}}\propto\mathbf{R}^{\top}\bm{\Sigma}^{\frac{1}{2}}{\bm{V}}^{\top} where 𝑽,𝚺

𝑽𝚺{\bm{V}},\bm{\Sigma} are SVD factors of 𝑳avgmmsubscriptsuperscript𝑳mmavg\bm{L}^{\rm{mm}}\_{\rm{avg}}. Since the j,j′

𝑗superscript𝑗′j,j^{\prime} columns of 𝑳avgmmsubscriptsuperscript𝑳mmavg\bm{L}^{\rm{mm}}\_{\rm{avg}} are the same by construction, the same is true for the j,j′

𝑗superscript𝑗′j,j^{\prime} columns of 𝑽⊤superscript𝑽top{\bm{V}}^{\top}. This proves the desired.

## Appendix C Additional discussions on the solution of [NTP-SVM⋆subscriptNTP-SVM⋆\text{NTP-SVM}\_{\star}](#S4.Ex6 "In Definition 3. ‣ 4.1 NTP-SVM logits ‣ 4 Analysis of unconstrained features model for NTP training ‣ Implicit Geometry of Next-token Prediction: From Language Sparsity Patterns to Model Representations")

In this section, we complement the results of Sec. [4.4](#S4.SS4 "4.4 On the solution of \"NTP-SVM\"_⋆ ‣ 4 Analysis of unconstrained features model for NTP training ‣ Implicit Geometry of Next-token Prediction: From Language Sparsity Patterns to Model Representations") on the properties of the [NTP-SVM⋆subscriptNTP-SVM⋆\text{NTP-SVM}\_{\star}](#S4.Ex6 "In Definition 3. ‣ 4.1 NTP-SVM logits ‣ 4 Analysis of unconstrained features model for NTP training ‣ Implicit Geometry of Next-token Prediction: From Language Sparsity Patterns to Model Representations") solution.

### C.1 Special case: support sets of equal sizes

Recall that in Prop. [2](#Thmproposition2 "Proposition 2. ‣ 4.4.1 Special case: support sets of equal sizes ‣ 4.4 On the solution of \"NTP-SVM\"_⋆ ‣ 4 Analysis of unconstrained features model for NTP training ‣ Implicit Geometry of Next-token Prediction: From Language Sparsity Patterns to Model Representations"), we introduced a special case of the sparsity pattern of the support sets for which we could find the optimal solution of [NTP-SVM⋆subscriptNTP-SVM⋆\text{NTP-SVM}\_{\star}](#S4.Ex6 "In Definition 3. ‣ 4.1 NTP-SVM logits ‣ 4 Analysis of unconstrained features model for NTP training ‣ Implicit Geometry of Next-token Prediction: From Language Sparsity Patterns to Model Representations") in closed-form. The proposition below provides a more detailed characterization of the implicit geometry of the embeddings.

###### Proposition 5.

Assume the setting of Proposition [2](#Thmproposition2 "Proposition 2. ‣ 4.4.1 Special case: support sets of equal sizes ‣ 4.4 On the solution of \"NTP-SVM\"_⋆ ‣ 4 Analysis of unconstrained features model for NTP training ‣ Implicit Geometry of Next-token Prediction: From Language Sparsity Patterns to Model Representations"). The geometry of context and word embeddings is described by the following relations:

### C.2 A simple candidate solution

Recall that in Sec. [4.4](#S4.SS4 "4.4 On the solution of \"NTP-SVM\"_⋆ ‣ 4 Analysis of unconstrained features model for NTP training ‣ Implicit Geometry of Next-token Prediction: From Language Sparsity Patterns to Model Representations"), we introduced 𝑺~=(𝕀V−1V​𝟙V​𝟙V⊤)​𝑺~𝑺subscript𝕀𝑉1𝑉subscript1𝑉superscriptsubscript1𝑉top𝑺\widetilde{\bm{S}}=(\mathds{I}\_{V}-\frac{1}{V}\mathds{1}\_{V}\mathds{1}\_{V}^{\top})\bm{S} as a candidate solution for the [NTP-SVM⋆subscriptNTP-SVM⋆\text{NTP-SVM}\_{\star}](#S4.Ex6 "In Definition 3. ‣ 4.1 NTP-SVM logits ‣ 4 Analysis of unconstrained features model for NTP training ‣ Implicit Geometry of Next-token Prediction: From Language Sparsity Patterns to Model Representations"). Further recall that Prop. [3](#Thmproposition3 "Proposition 3. ‣ 4.4.2 A simple candidate solution ‣ 4.4 On the solution of \"NTP-SVM\"_⋆ ‣ 4 Analysis of unconstrained features model for NTP training ‣ Implicit Geometry of Next-token Prediction: From Language Sparsity Patterns to Model Representations") provides a dual certificate condition that specifies whether 𝑺~~𝑺\widetilde{\bm{S}} is optimal. Our empirical evaluations show that 𝑺~~𝑺\widetilde{\bm{S}} successfully solves [NTP-SVM⋆subscriptNTP-SVM⋆\text{NTP-SVM}\_{\star}](#S4.Ex6 "In Definition 3. ‣ 4.1 NTP-SVM logits ‣ 4 Analysis of unconstrained features model for NTP training ‣ Implicit Geometry of Next-token Prediction: From Language Sparsity Patterns to Model Representations") in numerous realizations of 𝑺𝑺\bm{S} we have examined. However, this is not the case in all realizations of the support set structure.

In Fig. [9](#A3.F9 "Figure 9 ‣ C.2 A simple candidate solution ‣ Appendix C Additional discussions on the solution of \"NTP-SVM\"_⋆ ‣ Implicit Geometry of Next-token Prediction: From Language Sparsity Patterns to Model Representations"), we present an example of two realizations of 𝑺𝑺\bm{S}, where the condition in Prop. [3](#Thmproposition3 "Proposition 3. ‣ 4.4.2 A simple candidate solution ‣ 4.4 On the solution of \"NTP-SVM\"_⋆ ‣ 4 Analysis of unconstrained features model for NTP training ‣ Implicit Geometry of Next-token Prediction: From Language Sparsity Patterns to Model Representations") is met in one instance but not in the other. The second and third column of the figure display the heatmap of 𝑺~~𝑺\widetilde{\bm{S}} and the optimal solution of [NTP-SVM⋆subscriptNTP-SVM⋆\text{NTP-SVM}\_{\star}](#S4.Ex6 "In Definition 3. ‣ 4.1 NTP-SVM logits ‣ 4 Analysis of unconstrained features model for NTP training ‣ Implicit Geometry of Next-token Prediction: From Language Sparsity Patterns to Model Representations") as determined by CVXPY (Grant & Boyd, [2014](#bib.bib21)) for the support sets 𝑺𝑺\bm{S} shown in the first column. Notably, although 𝑺~~𝑺\widetilde{\bm{S}} is not optimal in the second row, it provides a close and simple approximation of the optimal 𝑳mmsuperscript𝑳mm\bm{L}^{\rm{mm}} which almost captures the overall structure of the optimal solution found by CVXPY. Additionally, in Fig. [10](#A3.F10 "Figure 10 ‣ C.2 A simple candidate solution ‣ Appendix C Additional discussions on the solution of \"NTP-SVM\"_⋆ ‣ Implicit Geometry of Next-token Prediction: From Language Sparsity Patterns to Model Representations"), we show similar heatmaps of the optimal logits for the setup of the Simplified TinyStories dataset in Sec. [5](#S5 "5 Experiments ‣ Implicit Geometry of Next-token Prediction: From Language Sparsity Patterns to Model Representations"). Once again, while 𝑺~~𝑺\widetilde{\bm{S}} is not necessarily optimal, it serves as a close proxy for the optimal solution. Note that in the case of one-hot classification under STEP imbalanced data, 𝑺~~𝑺\widetilde{\bm{S}} is previously shown to be the unique minimizer of [NTP-SVM⋆subscriptNTP-SVM⋆\text{NTP-SVM}\_{\star}](#S4.Ex6 "In Definition 3. ‣ 4.1 NTP-SVM logits ‣ 4 Analysis of unconstrained features model for NTP training ‣ Implicit Geometry of Next-token Prediction: From Language Sparsity Patterns to Model Representations") (Thrampoulidis et al., [2022](#bib.bib63)).

![Refer to caption](/html/2408.15417/assets/x15.png)

We remark the following about the dual problem derived above. First, because 𝑺~~𝑺\widetilde{\bm{S}} is feasible in [NTP-SVM⋆subscriptNTP-SVM⋆\text{NTP-SVM}\_{\star}](#S4.Ex6 "In Definition 3. ‣ 4.1 NTP-SVM logits ‣ 4 Analysis of unconstrained features model for NTP training ‣ Implicit Geometry of Next-token Prediction: From Language Sparsity Patterns to Model Representations") and constraints are linear, Slater’s conditions hold, thus, we have strong duality. Second, by complementary slackness, if the nonegativity conditions 𝑨​[v,j]≤0,v∉𝒮jformulae-sequence𝑨𝑣𝑗0𝑣subscript𝒮𝑗{\bm{A}}[v,j]\leq 0,v\notin{\mathcal{S}}\_{j} are strict, then, at optimality, 𝑳𝑳\bm{L} satisfies the inequality constraints ([10](#S4.E10 "In Definition 3. ‣ 4.1 NTP-SVM logits ‣ 4 Analysis of unconstrained features model for NTP training ‣ Implicit Geometry of Next-token Prediction: From Language Sparsity Patterns to Model Representations")) with equality.

Now, regarding solving the dual, observe that if we remove the constraint 𝑨​[v,j]≤0,v∉𝒮jformulae-sequence𝑨𝑣𝑗0𝑣subscript𝒮𝑗{\bm{A}}[v,j]\leq 0,v\notin{\mathcal{S}}\_{j}, then the solution to the dual is simply 𝑨=𝑼​𝑽⊤𝑨𝑼superscript𝑽top{\bm{A}}=\bm{U}{\bm{V}}^{\top} where 𝑼​𝚺​𝑽⊤𝑼𝚺superscript𝑽top\bm{U}\bm{\Sigma}{\bm{V}}^{\top} is the SVD of 𝑺~~𝑺\widetilde{\bm{S}}. In fact, it is not hard to show that this is the unique solution of the relaxed dual; see for example (Thrampoulidis et al., [2022](#bib.bib63), Lemma C.1) for an analogous result. Therefore, if 𝑼​𝑽⊤𝑼superscript𝑽top\bm{U}{\bm{V}}^{\top} satisfies the condition of the proposition, then it is clearly the unique optimal solution to the dual problem.

From this and complementary slackness discussed above, it must be that any minimizer of [NTP-SVM⋆subscriptNTP-SVM⋆\text{NTP-SVM}\_{\star}](#S4.Ex6 "In Definition 3. ‣ 4.1 NTP-SVM logits ‣ 4 Analysis of unconstrained features model for NTP training ‣ Implicit Geometry of Next-token Prediction: From Language Sparsity Patterns to Model Representations") must satisfy the inequality constraints with equalities. Formally, the optimal solution 𝑳^^𝑳\hat{\bm{L}} satisfies simultaneously:

Note that this is a system of m+∑j∈[m](Sj+V−1−Sj)=V​m𝑚subscript𝑗delimited-[]𝑚subscript𝑆𝑗𝑉1subscript𝑆𝑗𝑉𝑚m+\sum\_{j\in[m]}(S\_{j}+V-1-S\_{j})=Vm linear equations. It can also be easily verified that these equations are linearly independent. Thus, their unique solution is 𝑺~~𝑺\widetilde{\bm{S}}. This completes the proof of the proposition.

### C.4 Proof of Propositions [2](#Thmproposition2 "Proposition 2. ‣ 4.4.1 Special case: support sets of equal sizes ‣ 4.4 On the solution of \"NTP-SVM\"_⋆ ‣ 4 Analysis of unconstrained features model for NTP training ‣ Implicit Geometry of Next-token Prediction: From Language Sparsity Patterns to Model Representations") and [5](#Thmproposition5 "Proposition 5. ‣ C.1 Special case: support sets of equal sizes ‣ Appendix C Additional discussions on the solution of \"NTP-SVM\"_⋆ ‣ Implicit Geometry of Next-token Prediction: From Language Sparsity Patterns to Model Representations")

At the heart of proving both Propositions [2](#Thmproposition2 "Proposition 2. ‣ 4.4.1 Special case: support sets of equal sizes ‣ 4.4 On the solution of \"NTP-SVM\"_⋆ ‣ 4 Analysis of unconstrained features model for NTP training ‣ Implicit Geometry of Next-token Prediction: From Language Sparsity Patterns to Model Representations") and [5](#Thmproposition5 "Proposition 5. ‣ C.1 Special case: support sets of equal sizes ‣ Appendix C Additional discussions on the solution of \"NTP-SVM\"_⋆ ‣ Implicit Geometry of Next-token Prediction: From Language Sparsity Patterns to Model Representations") is the following result, which determines the SVD factors 𝑼𝑼\bm{U}, 𝚺𝚺\bm{\Sigma}, and 𝑽𝑽{\bm{V}} of 𝑺~=(𝕀V−1V​𝟙𝟙⊤)​𝑺~𝑺subscript𝕀𝑉1𝑉superscript11top𝑺\widetilde{\bm{S}}=(\mathds{I}\_{V}-\frac{1}{V}\mathds{1}\mathds{1}^{\top})\bm{S}. This shows immediately that word embeddings form an ETF. It also forms the basis for computing the geometry of context embeddings. We show this after proving the lemma.

###### Proof.

We will compute explicitly 𝑺~​𝑺~⊤~𝑺superscript~𝑺top\widetilde{\bm{S}}{\widetilde{\bm{S}}}^{\top}. Start with computing 𝑺​𝑺⊤𝑺superscript𝑺top\bm{S}\bm{S}^{\top}. For diagonal elements, the dot product is between a row of 𝑺𝑺\bm{S} and itself, counts the number of 111s in that row. Since each element is included in (V−1k−1)binomial𝑉1𝑘1\binom{V-1}{k-1} support sets (choosing the remaining k−1𝑘1k-1 elements from the other V−1𝑉1V-1 elements), each diagonal entry of 𝑺​𝑺⊤𝑺superscript𝑺top\bm{S}\bm{S}^{\top} is (V−1k−1)binomial𝑉1𝑘1\binom{V-1}{k-1}. For off-diagonal elements, the dot product counts the number of support sets in which both elements of the corresponding rows appear. This requires choosing the remaining k−2𝑘2k-2 elements from V−2𝑉2V-2 elements, giving (V−2k−2)binomial𝑉2𝑘2\binom{V-2}{k-2} for each off-diagonal entry of 𝑺​𝑺′𝑺superscript𝑺′\bm{S}\bm{S}^{\prime}. ## Appendix D Numerical experiments

### D.1 Additional details and results: Sec. [5](#S5 "5 Experiments ‣ Implicit Geometry of Next-token Prediction: From Language Sparsity Patterns to Model Representations")

#### D.1.1 Datasets

We use a total of three datasets. We first employ two smaller-scale datasets, which are well-suited for verifying our theoretical solutions. Then, we use one larger-scale dataset designed to investigate the geometric properties of text in conditions that approximate real-world text scenarios.

Synthetic. We generate each context 𝒙¯jsubscript¯𝒙𝑗\bar{\bm{x}}\_{j} as follows: We select T−1=5𝑇15T-1=5 words (tokens), and manually come up with the T=6𝑇6T=6-th tokens that are consistent with the given context. To model the probabilities π^jsubscript^𝜋𝑗\hat{\pi}\_{j} and 𝒑^jsubscript^𝒑𝑗\hat{\bm{p}}\_{j}, we sample each support independently to a number of repeats to emulate the behavior of repeated context in natural language. For instance, the context 𝒙¯j=subscript¯𝒙𝑗absent\bar{\bm{x}}\_{j}= ‘‘Lily wants to try the’’ is followed by 𝒮={soup,game,movie}𝒮soupgamemovie{\mathcal{S}}=\{\texttt{soup},\texttt{game},\texttt{movie}\} with respective probabilities [0.5,0.25,0.25]

0.50.250.25[0.5,0.25,0.25]. The dataset consists of n=116𝑛116n=116 samples, containing m=16𝑚16m=16 distinct contexts, and a vocabulary size of V=30𝑉30V=30. Each context has a fixed support set length of |𝒮j|=3subscript𝒮𝑗3|\mathcal{S}\_{j}|=3. In Fig. [11](#A4.F11 "Figure 11 ‣ D.1.1 Datasets ‣ D.1 Additional details and results: Sec. 5 ‣ Appendix D Numerical experiments ‣ Implicit Geometry of Next-token Prediction: From Language Sparsity Patterns to Model Representations"), we show the m=16𝑚16m=16 unique contexts and the soft labels on their next token. In this dataset, the tokenization is done at the word level and the empirical entropy lower-bound is ℋ=1.6597ℋ1.6597\mathcal{H}=1.6597.

![Refer to caption](/html/2408.15417/assets/arxiv_figs_yize/dataset.png)


Figure 11: The contexts, support sets, and soft labels in the Synthetic dataset.

Simplified TinyStories. Advancing towards a more realistic but still controlled dataset, we use contexts and support sets from the TinyStories corpus.
We derive contexts 𝒙¯jsubscript¯𝒙𝑗\bar{\bm{x}}\_{j} and support sets 𝒮jsubscript𝒮𝑗{\mathcal{S}}\_{j} from the TinyStories dataset and we create the training data from the most frequent word-level contexts with length 5, such as 𝒙¯j=[‘‘once’’, ‘‘upon’’, ‘‘a’’, ‘‘time’’, ‘‘,’’]subscript¯𝒙𝑗delimited-[]‘‘once’’, ‘‘upon’’, ‘‘a’’, ‘‘time’’, ‘‘,’’\bar{\bm{x}}\_{j}=[\texttt{``once'', ``upon'', ``a'', ``time'', ``,''}]. For the support sets 𝒮jsubscript𝒮𝑗{\mathcal{S}}\_{j}, we record all next tokens of 𝒙¯jsubscript¯𝒙𝑗\bar{\bm{x}}\_{j} in the original dataset. Then, we replace the words in the contexts with their synonyms to generate new contexts with identical support sets. This allows us to have multiple contexts sharing common support sets, while controlling the vocabulary size, which is set to V=104𝑉104V=104. The final dataset consists of n≈3050𝑛3050n\approx 3050 samples with m≈400𝑚400m\approx 400 unique contexts. The frequencies 𝒑^jsubscript^𝒑𝑗\hat{\bm{p}}\_{j} are determined by independently sampling each next token from 𝒮jsubscript𝒮𝑗{\mathcal{S}}\_{j} several times. Here, ℋ=1.0166ℋ1.0166\mathcal{H}=1.0166.

TinyStories. To experiment with more a standard dataset, we use 100 stories sampled from TinyStories. Here, we do not make any sample selections for contexts and support sets, unlike the previously mentioned datasets. We use a tokenizer with vocabulary size V=128𝑉128V=128, and fixed context length T−1=6𝑇16T-1=6. We choose a small context window and vocabulary size to make tracking the distinct contexts and their support sets computationally manageable. In this setup, the number of distinct contexts m∼105similar-to𝑚superscript105m\sim 10^{5} and ℋ=0.3112ℋ0.3112\mathcal{H}=0.3112.

#### D.1.2 Training details

Synthetic and TinyStories experiemtns. We train the models long enough to ensure that the loss approached the empirical entropy lower-bound ℋℋ\mathcal{H} within an order of 10−4superscript10410^{-4}. In all experiments, we use AdamW optimizer with a weight decay λ=10−6𝜆superscript106\lambda=10^{-6}. The learning rate is initially set to 10−4superscript10410^{-4}, with a step-decay schedule.

TinyStories experiemtns. We train TF with AdamW optimizer until it reaches the empirical entropy lower-bound within an order 10−3superscript10310^{-3}. We use warm-up over the first 5 epochs to increase the learning rate to 5×10−45superscript1045\times 10^{-4} and use a cosine learning rate scheduler to decay its values over the course of the training. We set the weight decay to 10−5superscript10510^{-5}.

#### D.1.3 Additional results

Figs. [12](#A4.F12 "Figure 12 ‣ D.1.3 Additional results ‣ D.1 Additional details and results: Sec. 5 ‣ Appendix D Numerical experiments ‣ Implicit Geometry of Next-token Prediction: From Language Sparsity Patterns to Model Representations")-[15](#A4.F15 "Figure 15 ‣ D.1.3 Additional results ‣ D.1 Additional details and results: Sec. 5 ‣ Appendix D Numerical experiments ‣ Implicit Geometry of Next-token Prediction: From Language Sparsity Patterns to Model Representations") complement the discussions and experimental results in Sec. [5](#S5 "5 Experiments ‣ Implicit Geometry of Next-token Prediction: From Language Sparsity Patterns to Model Representations"). Below we only discuss the missing details of the TinyStories experiments.

Visualization details of TinyStories experiments. For visualizing the heatmap of embeddings similarity in Fig. [2](#S1.F2 "Figure 2 ‣ 1.2 Summary of findings ‣ 1 Introduction ‣ Implicit Geometry of Next-token Prediction: From Language Sparsity Patterns to Model Representations"), we choose 101010 unique support sets in the dataset with |𝒮j|>2subscript𝒮𝑗2|{\mathcal{S}}\_{j}|>2. For each of the chosen support sets 𝒮𝒮{\mathcal{S}}, we choose 101010 distinct contexts j𝑗j such that 𝒮j=𝒮subscript𝒮𝑗𝒮{\mathcal{S}}\_{j}={\mathcal{S}} (a total of 100100100 samples), and illustrate the Gram matrix of the centered support matrix 𝑺~~𝑺\widetilde{\bm{S}} and context embeddings 𝑯𝑯{\bm{H}} for this subset of the contexts. Given that the support sets are sparse, we choose to use contexts with the largest support set size which would result in a more pronounced pattern on the correlation matrices. We skip visualizing the word embeddings 𝑾𝑾{\bm{W}} in this experiment, as the heatmaps did not display any noticeable visual structures.

For the metrics displayed in Fig. [6](#S5.F6 "Figure 6 ‣ 5.3 Metrics. ‣ 5 Experiments ‣ Implicit Geometry of Next-token Prediction: From Language Sparsity Patterns to Model Representations"), due to the larger number of context embeddings, we only compute the metrics such as ∥𝒫ℱ​(𝑳k)−𝑳in∥delimited-∥∥subscript𝒫ℱsubscript𝑳𝑘superscript𝑳in\lVert\mathcal{P}\_{\mathscr{F}}(\bm{L}\_{k})-\bm{L}^{\rm{in}}\rVert, norm growth and correlation with proxies using a sample of 100010001000 token/context pairs chosen randomly from the training set. We observe qualitatively similar behavior compared to other experiments, with the exception of the norm growth of 𝑯𝑯{\bm{H}}, which we conjecture to be a consequence of layer normalization in the final embedding layer.

### D.2 Other architectures

The results in Thms. [1](#Thmtheorem1 "Theorem 1. ‣ 4.2 Regularization-path analysis of logits ‣ 4 Analysis of unconstrained features model for NTP training ‣ Implicit Geometry of Next-token Prediction: From Language Sparsity Patterns to Model Representations") and [2](#Thmtheorem2 "Theorem 2. ‣ 4.2 Regularization-path analysis of logits ‣ 4 Analysis of unconstrained features model for NTP training ‣ Implicit Geometry of Next-token Prediction: From Language Sparsity Patterns to Model Representations") hold provided the model is expressive enough to generate (approximately) unconstrained embeddings and the loss can be minimized close to the lower-bound. Depending on the expressiveness of a specific network design, the size of the network required for achieving the entropy lower-bound can vary significantly.

To explore this, we hereby repeat our experiments on Simplified TinyStories by replacing the TF model with a multi-layer perceptron (MLP). We use an MLP consisting of 20 layers organized into four blocks, each containing five layers, with hidden dimension sizes 1024, 512, 256, and 128. This leads to a network with around 888 times more parameters than the TF model. The final embeddings geometry is depicted in Fig. [16](#A4.F16 "Figure 16 ‣ D.2 Other architectures ‣ Appendix D Numerical experiments ‣ Implicit Geometry of Next-token Prediction: From Language Sparsity Patterns to Model Representations"). We observe that the geometrical patterns appearing in corr​(𝑯)corr𝑯\textsc{corr}({\bm{H}}) and corr​(𝑾⊤)corrsuperscript𝑾top\textsc{corr}({\bm{W}}^{\top}) at a coarse level are similar to those of the TF model. However, the MLP, even with ∼10similar-toabsent10\sim 10 times larger size, struggles to recover the fine-grained patterns. In terms of loss convergence, we find the MLP converges to the empirical entropy in the order of 10−2superscript10210^{-2} but TF converges in the order of 10−4superscript10410^{-4} on the same dataset. We conjecture that an even larger MLP might be able to achieve better convergence to our theoretical prediction, but we also caution of potential optimization bottlenecks. We encourage additional experiments with other architectures such as LSTMs or state-space models as future work.

See App. [D.2](#A4.SS2 "D.2 Other architectures ‣ Appendix D Numerical experiments ‣ Implicit Geometry of Next-token Prediction: From Language Sparsity Patterns to Model Representations") for details.

### D.3 Auto-regressive training

Here, we train the model auto-regressively. In the previous settings, we focused on training samples that were all of fixed length T−1=5𝑇15T-1=5. In this section, we let the model learn the embeddings for different sequence lengths 1≤T−1≤161𝑇1161\leq T-1\leq 16. Note that the length of the context does not have an impact on our theoretical analysis other than affecting the sparsity pattern 𝑺𝑺\bm{S} of the training set which in turn influences the implicit geometry as we have seen.

We train an 8-layer TF on 200 stories from TinyStories using character-level tokenizer, which limits the vocabulary to approximately 40 characters and promotes higher entropy in next-token distribution. We denote the loss value across different positions T∈{2,⋯,17}𝑇2⋯17T\in\{2,\cdots,17\} at iteration k𝑘k by CE⁡(𝑳T,k)CEsubscript𝑳

𝑇𝑘\operatorname{CE}(\bm{L}\_{T,k}). In Fig. [18](#A4.F18 "Figure 18 ‣ D.3 Auto-regressive training ‣ Appendix D Numerical experiments ‣ Implicit Geometry of Next-token Prediction: From Language Sparsity Patterns to Model Representations"), we display the distance of each loss component CE⁡(𝑳T,k)CEsubscript𝑳

𝑇𝑘\operatorname{CE}(\bm{L}\_{T,k}) from its empirical entropy lower-bound ℋTsubscriptℋ𝑇\mathcal{H}\_{T}, i.e., the T-gram entropy of the training set. We observe generally better convergence for shorter sequence lengths T𝑇T.
In Fig. [17](#A4.F17 "Figure 17 ‣ D.3 Auto-regressive training ‣ Appendix D Numerical experiments ‣ Implicit Geometry of Next-token Prediction: From Language Sparsity Patterns to Model Representations"), we illustrate the context embeddings similarities. For visualization, we select 5 contexts per each sequence length that end in token “y\_” or “\_t”. The context embeddings and the proxy [(P)](#S1.I3.ix1 "item (P) ‣ 1.2 Summary of findings ‣ 1 Introduction ‣ Implicit Geometry of Next-token Prediction: From Language Sparsity Patterns to Model Representations") show similar patterns at a coarse level. However, at finer scale, the learned context embeddings that end with the same tokens exhibit strong correlation on average, even when their support sets do not align. We defer further investigation into whether the embeddings’ dependence is due to insufficient network capacity or an optimization bottleneck to future work.

### D.4 Discussion on large vocabulary setting

Throughout our analysis, we require the embedding dimension to be larger than the vocabulary size, i.e., d≥V𝑑𝑉d\geq V. This condition was necessary to make the non-convex problem in ([8](#S3.E8 "In Lemma 1. ‣ 3.3 Reformulation in terms of logits ‣ 3 Formulation ‣ Implicit Geometry of Next-token Prediction: From Language Sparsity Patterns to Model Representations")) convex.
However, if there exists a low-rank optimal solution in [NTP-SVM⋆subscriptNTP-SVM⋆\text{NTP-SVM}\_{\star}](#S4.Ex6 "In Definition 3. ‣ 4.1 NTP-SVM logits ‣ 4 Analysis of unconstrained features model for NTP training ‣ Implicit Geometry of Next-token Prediction: From Language Sparsity Patterns to Model Representations"), i.e., rank⁡(𝑳mm)<Vranksuperscript𝑳mm𝑉\operatorname{rank}\left(\bm{L}^{\rm{mm}}\right)<V, the condition d≥V𝑑𝑉d\geq V can be relaxed in the analysis. In general, the smallest rank among all minimizers depends on the sparsity pattern of the language. It is intriguing to investigate this dependence further.

This section examines the network’s performance when the decoder dimension d𝑑d is smaller than the vocabulary size V𝑉V. To ensure a fair comparison between different setups, we fix the transformer blocks’ inner embedding dimension dTF=64subscript𝑑TF64d\_{\text{TF}}=64 to maintain comparable expressivity for all networks. To vary the final layer embedding dimension d𝑑d, we add an additional linear layer on top of the network to adjust the context embedding dimension.

In Fig. [19](#A4.F19 "Figure 19 ‣ D.4 Discussion on large vocabulary setting ‣ Appendix D Numerical experiments ‣ Implicit Geometry of Next-token Prediction: From Language Sparsity Patterns to Model Representations"), we show the learned context embeddings for d=128𝑑128d=128 and d=64𝑑64d=64, for a 10-layer TF trained on the Simplified TinyStories dataset with V=104𝑉104V=104. We observe that with smaller d𝑑d, the speed of convergence to the entropy lower-bound ℋℋ\mathcal{H} decreases. However, the learned context embeddings exhibit relatively similar geometry with moderate values of d<V𝑑𝑉d<V. We leave more theoretical and empirical exploration of this setting to future works.

// Auxiliary function, building the preview feature when
// an inline citation is clicked
function clicked\_cite(e) {
e.preventDefault();
let cite = this.closest('.ltx\_cite');
let next = cite.nextSibling;
if (next && next.nodeType == Node.ELEMENT\_NODE && next.getAttribute('class') == "ar5iv-bibitem-preview") {
next.remove();
return; }
// Before adding a preview modal,
// cleanup older previews, in case they're still open
document.querySelectorAll('span.ar5iv-bibitem-preview').forEach(function(node) {
node.remove();
})
// Create the preview
preview = document.createElement('span');
preview.setAttribute('class','ar5iv-bibitem-preview');
let target = document.getElementById(this.getAttribute('href').slice(1));
target.childNodes.forEach(function (child) {
preview.append(child.cloneNode(true));
});
let close\_x = document.createElement('button');
close\_x.setAttribute("aria-label","Close modal for bibliography item preview");
close\_x.textContent = "×";
close\_x.setAttribute('class', 'ar5iv-button-close-preview');
close\_x.setAttribute('onclick','this.parentNode.remove()');
preview.append(close\_x);
preview.querySelectorAll('.ltx\_tag\_bibitem').forEach(function(node) {
node.remove();
});
cite.parentNode.insertBefore(preview, cite.nextSibling);
return;
}
// Global Document initialization:
// - assign the preview feature to all inline citation links
document.querySelectorAll(".ltx\_cite .ltx\_ref").forEach(function (link) {
link.addEventListener("click", clicked\_cite);
});
